Full Step Functions Execution Support
I didn't see this in the issues, so my apologies if it's already filed. 

This is a feature request for full Step Function execution support, such that test code could call sfn.StartExecution and get a production-like execution of a state machine, including retries, error handling, concurrency, choice evaluation, etc, and actually runs application code.
Something like StepFunctionsLocal from AWS, which is in java: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/step-functions/latest/dg/sfn-local.html

I appreciate that implementing all of step functions is a daunting task! I will describe a subset that I think would be useful to a large set of developers. 

My ideal implementation would support State Language and lambda task integration. 

I suppose of course it could it integrate with moto.lambda to run lambda functions, but I think some kind of configuration interface/escape hatch enabling test writers to implement their own implementation of any task type would keep it flexible enough for users to do some minimal work to keep up with new step functions features. 

In my case, I would hook the lambda tasks directly to my own worker code and skip the lambda worker model that moto has, as I don't use this

+    def send_task_success(self, task_token: str, outcome:<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Calculator` class in Python that supports basic arithmetic operations: addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. The class should include methods for each operation and handle division by zero by returning `None`. Additionally, implement a method `perform_operations(operations)` that processes a list of operations. Each operation is a string formatted as "operation operand1 operand2". The method should return a list of results corresponding to each operation. If an operation is invalid or cannot be performed, append `None` to the results list.

**Sample Input:**
```python
operations = ["add 5 3", "multiply 2 4", "divide 10 0", "subtract 7 2", "divide 5 2", "add 3 a"]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[8, 8, None, 5, 2.5, None]
```

```python
class Calculator:
    def add(self, a, b):
        """Return the sum of a and b."""
        return a + b

    def subtract(self, a, b):
        """Return the difference of a and b."""
        return a - b

    def multiply(self, a, b):
        """Return the product of a and b."""
        return a * b

    def divide(self, a, b):
        """Return the quotient of a and b, or None if b is zero."""
        if b == 0:
            return None
        return a / b

    def perform_operations(self, operations):
        """Process a list of operations and return a list of results."""
        results = []
        for operation in operations:
            parts = operation.split()
            if len(parts) != 3:
                results.append(None)
                continue

            operator = parts[0]
            try:
                a = float(parts[1])
                b = float(parts[2])
            except ValueError:
                results.append(None)
                continue

            if operator == "add":
                results.append(self.add(a, b))
            elif operator == "subtract":
                results.append(self.subtract(a, b))
            elif operator == "multiply":
                results.append(self.multiply(a, b))
            elif operator == "divide":
                results.append(self.divide(a, b))
            else:
                results.append(None)
        return results

+    status<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a function `filter_vowels(sentence)` that processes a given string `sentence` by removing all vowels, both uppercase and lowercase. The function should handle strings containing letters, punctuation, and spaces. Your solution should be case-insensitive.

**Input:**
- A single string `sentence` (1 ≤ |sentence| ≤ 1000), consisting of letters, punctuation, and spaces.

**Output:**
- A string with all vowels removed.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Hello, World!"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
"Hll, Wrld!"
```

```python
def filter_vowels(sentence):
    """
    Removes all vowels from the given string `sentence`.
    
    Parameters:
    sentence (str): The input string from which vowels will be removed.
    
    Returns:
    str: The resulting string with all vowels removed.
    """
    vowels = "aeiouAEIOU"
    return ''.join(char for char in sentence if char not in vowels)

Your task is to implement a function `check_palindrome(phrase)` that returns `True` if the string is a palindrome, considering only alphanumeric characters and ignoring case, and `False` otherwise. A palindrome is a string that reads the same forward and backward.

**Input:**
- A single string `phrase` (1 ≤ len(phrase) ≤ 1000).

**Output:**
- Return `True` if `phrase` is a palindrome under the given conditions, otherwise return `False`.

**Sample Input 1:**
```
"A man, a plan, a canal, Panama"
```
**Sample Output 1:**
```
True
```

**Sample Input 2:**
```
"race a car"
```
**Sample Output 2:**
```
False
```

```python
def check_palindrome(phrase):
    """
    Determines if the given string is a palindrome, considering only alphanumeric characters and ignoring case.

    :param phrase: A string to be checked.
    :return: True if the string is a palindrome, False otherwise.
    """
    cleaned_phrase = ''.join(char.lower() for char in phrase if char.isalnum())
    return cleaned_phrase == cleaned_phrase[::-1]

```python
def parse_contract(contract_str):
    trick = int(contract_str[0])
    remaining = contract_str[1:]
    if remaining.startswith('NT'):
        suit = 'NT'
        doubling_part = remaining[2:]
    else:
        suit = remaining[0]
        doubling_part = remaining[1:]
    return trick, suit, doubling_part

+class LambdaFunctionStartFailed<#sep#>You are given a list containing integers and strings. Your task is to write a Python function that calculates the sum of all even integers in the list. Non-integer values should be ignored. 

**Input:**
- A list of elements, where each element is either an integer or a string.

**Output:**
- An integer representing the sum of all even integers in the list.

**Sample Input:**
```
[1, 2, "three", 4, 5, 6, "seven", 8]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
20
```

```python
def sum_of_even_numbers(numbers):
    """
    Calculate the sum of all even integers in a list, ignoring non-integer values.

    :param numbers: List of elements, where each element is either an integer or a string.
    :return: Integer sum of all even integers in the list.
    """
    total = 0
    for num in numbers:
        if isinstance(num, int) and num % 2 == 0:
            total += num
    return total

# Example usage:
numbers = [1, 2, "three", 4, 5, 6, "seven", 8]
print(sum_of_even_numbers(numbers))  # Output: 20
```<#sep#> powers of two can be 1 only if one is 1 and the other is 2. Which means one of them has exponent 1. So let's say k(y+1)=1, then since k and y are natural numbers, k=1 and y+1=1 ⇒ y=0, invalid.

### Case 1: \( 2^x = n^{y+1} + 1 \)

1. **Subcase: \( n = 1 \)**

   - The equation becomes \( 2^x - 1 = 1 \), which simplifies to \( 2^x = 2 \).

  * All suns on the image are circles.

The input limits for scoring 100 points are (subproblems F1+F2+F3):

  * All suns on the image are ellipses, they can be arbitrarily rotated. 

Output

Examples

+    stateMachineAliasArn<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a RESTful API to manage a collection of books. The API must support the following operations: create, read, update, and delete (CRUD). Your implementation should include the following endpoints:

- **Create a new book**: `POST /books`
- **Retrieve all books**: `GET /books`
- **Retrieve a specific book by ID**: `GET /books/<int:book_id>`
- **Update a book by ID**: `PUT /books/<int:book_id>`
- **Delete a book by ID**: `DELETE /books/<int:book_id>`

For each endpoint, specify the expected request and response formats. The API should handle errors gracefully, returning appropriate HTTP status codes.

**Sample Input/Output:**

1. **Create a new book**
   - Request: `POST /books` with JSON body `{"title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee"}`
   - Response: `201 Created` with JSON body `{"id": 3, "title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee"}`

2. **Retrieve all books**
   - Request: `GET /books`
   - Response: `200 OK` with JSON body `[{"id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell"}, {"id": 2, "title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald"}, {"id": 3, "title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee"}]`

3. **Retrieve a specific book by ID**
   - Request: `GET /books/1`
   - Response: `200 OK` with JSON body `{"id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell"}`

4. **Update a book by ID**
   - Request: `PUT /books/1` with JSON body `{"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell"}`
   - Response: `200 OK` with JSON body `{"id": 1, "title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell"}`

5. **Delete a book by ID**
   - Request: `DELETE /books/1`
   - Response: `200 OK` with JSON body `{"result": true}`

```python
from flask import Flask, jsonify, request, abort

app = Flask(__name__)

# In-memory storage for books
books = [
    {'id': 1, 'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell'},
    {'id': 2, 'title': 'The Great Gatsby', 'author': 'F. Scott Fitzgerald'}
]

def _find_book(book_id):
    """Find a book by its ID."""
    return next((book for book in books if book['id'] == book_id), None)

@app.route('/books', methods=['POST'])
def create_book():
    """Create a new book."""
    if not request.json or not 'title' in request.json or not 'author' in request.json:
        abort(400)
    new_book = {
        'id': books[-1]['id'] + 1 if books else 1,
        'title': request.json['title'],
        'author': request.json['author']
    }
    books.append(new_book)
    return jsonify(new_book), 201

@app.route('/books', methods=['GET'])
def get_books():
    """Retrieve all books."""
    return jsonify(books)

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_book(book_id):
    """Retrieve a specific book by ID."""
    book = _find_book(book_id)
    if book is None:
        abort(404)
    return jsonify(book)

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_book(book_id):
    """Update a book by ID."""
    book = _find_book(book_id)
    if book is None:
        abort(404)
    if not request.json:
        abort(400)
    book['title'] = request.json.get('title', book['title'])
    book['author'] = request.json.get('author', book['author'])
    return jsonify(book)

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_book(book_id):
    """Delete a book by ID."""
    book = _find_book(book_id)
    if book is None:
        abort(404)
    books.remove(book)
    return jsonify({'result': True})

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)
```<#sep#> - This gives us \( n^{y+1} = 3^2 = 9 \) and \( 2^x = 9 + 1 = 10 \), which is not a power of 2.
   - The only valid solution from Mihăilescu's theorem is \( n = 3 \), \( y+1 = 2 \), and \( 2^x = 3^2 + 1 = 9 + 1 = 10 \), but \( 10 \) is not a power of 2.
   - However, the correct interpretation is \( n = 3 \), \( y = 1 \), and \( x = 3 \), which gives the solution \( (3, 3, 1) \).

### Case 2: \( 2^x = n^{y+1} - 1 \)

1. **Subcase: \( n = 1 \)**
   - If \( n = 1 \), then \( n^{y+1} = 1 \) for any natural number \( y \).
   - The equation becomes \( 2^x - 1 = -1 \), which simplifies to \( 2^x = 0 \), which is not possible for natural numbers.

2. **Subcase: \( n > 1 \)**
   - We again use Mihăilescu's theorem.
   - The only solution from Mihăilescu's theorem is \( n = 3 \), \( y+1 = 2 \), and \( 2^x = 3^2 - 1 = 9 - 1 = 8 \).
   - This gives \( x = 3 \), which is the solution \( (3, 3, 1) \).

### Conclusion

- All triples \( (1, 1, y) \) for any natural number \( y \).

<#sep#>You are given a list of tuples, where each tuple represents the coordinates of a point in a 2D plane. Your task is to write a Python function that calculates and returns the Euclidean distance between the first and last points in the list. The Euclidean distance between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is defined as \(\sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}\).

**Input:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains two integers representing the x and y coordinates of a point.

**Output:**
- A float representing the Euclidean distance between the first and last points in the list. If the list is empty, return 0.0.

**Sample Input:**
```
[(1, 2), (3, 4), (5, 6)]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
5.656854249492381
```

```python
import math

def euclidean_distance(coords):
    """
    Calculate the Euclidean distance between the first and last points in a list of coordinates.

    :param coords: List of tuples, where each tuple contains two integers representing the x and y coordinates.
    :return: Float representing the Euclidean distance between the first and last points. Returns 0.0 if the list is empty.
    """
    if not coords:
        return 0.0
    first = coords[0]
    last = coords[-1]
    return math.sqrt((last[0] - first[0]) ** 2 + (last[1] - first[1]) ** 2)

# Example usage
coords = [(1, 2), (3, 4), (5, 6)]
print(euclidean_distance(coords))
```<#sep#>Azar and Carl play a game of tic-tac-toe. Azar places an $\textsf{X}$ in one of the boxes in a $3$-by-$3$ array of boxes, then Carl places an $\textsf{O}$ in one of the remaining boxes. +       <#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class to manage a library's inventory. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `ISBN`: A string representing the International Standard Book Number.
  - `year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
  - `copies`: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.

- **Methods:**
  - `check_out()`: Decrements the number of copies by 1 if there are copies available. Returns `True` if a copy was successfully checked out, otherwise returns `False`.
  - `check_in()`: Increments the number of copies by 1.
  - `is_available()`: Returns `True` if there are any copies available, otherwise returns `False`.

Implement the `Book` class with the constructor and the methods described above.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949, 3)
print(book.is_available())  # Output: True
print(book.check_out())     # Output: True
print(book.check_out())     # Output: True
print(book.check_out())     # Output: True
print(book.check_out())     # Output: False
print(book.is_available())  # Output: False
book.check_in()
print(book.is_available())  # Output: True
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, ISBN, year, copies):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param ISBN: The International Standard Book Number.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        :param copies: The number of copies available.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.ISBN = ISBN
        self.year = year
        self.copies = copies

    def check_out(self):
        """
        Checks out a copy of the book if available.

        :return: True if a copy was successfully checked out, False otherwise.
        """
        if self.copies > 0:
            self.copies -= 1
            return True
        else:
            return False

    def check_in(self):
        """
        Returns a copy of the book to the inventory.
        """
        self.copies += 1

    def is_available(self):
        """
        Checks if there are any copies of the book available.

        :return: True if copies are available, False otherwise.
        """
        return self.copies > 0
```<#sep#> place his three X's on the remaining cells without creating a winning line for himself before move 6.

Now implementing this requires skeletonization.

Now skeletonization techniques.

I remember that to compute the skeleton, one approach is via the medial axis transform, which can be calculated using Voronoi diagrams.

Alternatively, use a thinning algorithm.

Zhang-Suen thinning is an iterative algorithm that reduces the region to its skeleton. It's for 8-connected regions and works in pairs of steps (each step removing possible end points).

Let me recall the steps of Zhang-Suen:

The Zhang-Suen thinning algorithm is an iterative algorithm that works as follows:

1. Label the foreground pixels (the object to be skeletonized) as 1, background as 0.

2. In each iteration, there are two steps: first performing step 1 and then step 2.

3. In each step, pixels that satisfy certain conditions are removed, which is determined by their neighborhood.

Conditions are based on:

- Number of transitions from 0 to foreground (2 to 8 neighbors) → "B" variable.

- The number of foreground neighbors (2 neighbors in an 8-neighborhood, so N is the count) → "N".

The conditions for a pixel to be removed in step 1:

- 2 ≤ N ≤ 6

- The number of transitions of its neighbors is 1.

- The pixel's own position (over some cyclic order).

Similarly for step 2: conditions take a slightly different orientation (not sure of exact condition).

The algorithm iterates between these two steps until no more pixels are removed.

This requires a bit of code, but since this is a problem requiring programming in contest environment, this may be manageable but perhaps a bit involved.

Alternatively, for the programming competition problem's code, perhaps a BFS approach to find the skeleton?

Another Idea:

Alternatively, for each pixel in the component, compute its euclidian distance to the "background" (the non-component pixels). The skeletal points are local maxima in that distance field along each path from the pixel to the nearest background. 

Wait: another approach:

The skeleton of a shape is the set of points equidistant to two or more parts of the boundary of the shape.

But computing this needs the distance transform.

The "Distance Transform" assigns to each background point the distance to the nearest foreground pixel (but since we are interested in the skeleton, perhaps the distance from object's foreground to other foreground points)?

Wait, the distance transform here should be computed on the foreground object. So for each pixel in the object, compute their distance to the background?

Alternatively, the distance transform for the skeleton:

The skeleton can be found as the set of pixels that are centers of circles that are maximal inradius and entirely within the shape.

Hmm, to approximate:

Calculate for each pixel in the region, the distance to the nearest edge (i.e., the closest point where the region ends). Then, the medial axis consists of those pixels where this local distance is a local maximum along all paths in the region.

This way, the skeleton can be found as the set of points where all their immediate neighbors have shorter distances.

Computing such local maxima could be done as follows:

Let me denote for each pixel (x,y), d(x,y) is the distance from (x,y) to the nearest edge of the component. (the edge being pixels adjacent to background).

Then the skeleton points are those points where d(x,y) is greater than all their 8 neighbors' d().

Wait, if a point's distance is a local maximum, then it is part of the medial axis.

Yes, that's another way to compute the skeleton.

Yes! The medial axis for a 2D region can be approximated by those points where the distance to the boundary is locally maximum.

So the steps are:

Compute for each pixel the shortest distance to the boundary of the region (i.e., edge pixels). 

Then, the skeleton consists of those pixels where their distance is higher than all their 8 neighbors.

That could work.

This seems feasible computationally:

1. Precompute the distance (d(x,y)) of each pixel (x,y) in the region to the nearest background pixel (the boundary). 

2. Then, for each such (x,y), if d(x,y) is a local maximum in all 8 directions, then this is part of the skeleton.

However, this requires computing the distance to the nearest edge, and then a local maxima.

Computing the distance map for each region.

Computing the distance to the edge can be done via BFS starting from all edge pixels of the region, then doing a reverse distance.

The steps to find d(x,y) for each pixel in region are:

- For the current region, first find all boundary pixels (those adjacent to a background pixel).

- Then, perform a BFS where the starting set is all the boundary pixels. The distance to each interior pixel is the distance from the nearest boundary. Wait, but that way, d(x,y) represents the distance to the boundary (edge) of the region.

Wait, for example, a point lying in the center of the region would have a high distance (the maximum).

Then, the medial axis points (skeleton) are the points where d(x,y) is a local peak. 

So in code:

For a region's mask (assuming 1 for region, 0 else.)

Steps:

Find all boundary pixels (those with neighbor(s) of 0).

Initialize a distance matrix, filled with -1 (unvisited). 

Set all boundary pixels to have d=0.

Then perform a multi-source BFS:

Each step, for each pixel, d(x,y) is the distance from the boundary. 

Once the BFS is complete, every pixel in the region has their distance.

Once this is done, compute skeleton matrix: 

Sk(x,y) is true if d(x,y) > d(nx, ny) for ALL 8 neighbors (nx,ny).

+<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `account_number`: A string representing the account number.
  - `holder_name`: A string representing the name of the account holder.
  - `balance`: A float representing the current balance in the account, initialized to 0 if not provided.

- **Methods:**
  - `deposit(amount)`: Increases the balance by the specified amount if the amount is positive.
  - `withdraw(amount)`: Decreases the balance by the specified amount if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
  - `get_balance()`: Returns the current balance of the account.

- **Class Method:**
  - `total_accounts()`: Returns the total number of `BankAccount` instances created.

**Sample Input/Output:**

- **Input:**
  ```
  account1 = BankAccount("1234567890", "John Doe", 1000)
  account2 = BankAccount("0987654321", "Jane Smith", 500)
  account1.deposit(200)
  account1.withdraw(300)
  account2.deposit(150)
  account2.withdraw(2000)
  account3 = BankAccount("1122334455", "Alice Johnson", 200)
  ```

- **Output:**
  ```
  Deposited 200. New balance is 1200.
  Withdrew 300. New balance is 900.
  Deposited 150. New balance is 650.
  Insufficient funds.
  ```

- **Additional Output:**
  ```
  Account 1 balance: 900
  Account 2 balance: 650
  Total number of bank accounts: 3
  ```

```python
class BankAccount:
    _total_accounts = 0  # Class variable to track the number of bank accounts

    def __init__(self, account_number, holder_name, balance=0):
        """
        Initializes a new BankAccount instance.

        :param account_number: A string representing the account number.
        :param holder_name: A string representing the name of the account holder.
        :param balance: A float representing the initial balance, default is 0.
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.holder_name = holder_name
        self.balance = balance
        BankAccount._total_accounts += 1  # Increment the total accounts on creation

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits a specified amount into the account if the amount is positive.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to deposit.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited {amount}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to withdraw.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.

        :return: A float representing the current balance.
        """
        return self.balance

    @classmethod
    def total_accounts(cls):
        """
        Returns the total number of BankAccount instances created.

        :return: An integer representing the total number of accounts.
        """
        return cls._total_accounts

# Example of creating multiple BankAccount instances and performing operations on them
account1 = BankAccount("1234567890", "John Doe", 1000)
account2 = BankAccount("0987654321", "Jane Smith", 500)

account1.deposit(200)
account1.withdraw(300)
print(f"Account 1 balance: {account1.get_balance()}")  # Should print 900

account2.deposit(150)
account2.withdraw(2000)  # Should print "Insufficient funds."
print(f"Account 2 balance: {account2.get_balance()}")  # Should print 650

account3 = BankAccount("1122334455", "Alice Johnson", 200)
print(f"Total number of bank accounts: {BankAccount.total_accounts()}")  # Should print 3

For a connected component:

1. Mark all its pixels (in a region mask).

2. Find all boundary pixels: for any pixel in the component, check if any of its 8 neighbors is 0 (background). The boundary pixels are those component pixels with at least one 8-neighbor outside.

3. Compute distance map from those boundary pixels.

   - Use a BFS queue starting from the boundary pixels, the distance to each pixel is the distance from boundary.

4. Once all distances are computed, then create skeleton by checking for each pixel if it's a local maxima of distance.

Then, the skeleton matrix has 1's for skeleton pixels.

for each skeleton pixel (x,y):

   count = 0

       if neighbor is also in skeleton:

+        0<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Counter` that maintains a count of how many times a specific method is invoked. The class should support the following operations:

- `increment()`: Increases the count by 1 each time it is called.
- `get_count()`: Returns the current count.
- `reset_count()`: Resets the count to zero.

The class should initialize the count to zero in its constructor `__init__`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Initialize `Counter` object.
- Call `increment()` twice.
- Call `get_count()`, expected output: `2`.
- Call `reset_count()`.
- Call `get_count()`, expected output: `0`.

```python
class Counter:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize the count to zero."""
        self.count = 0

    def increment(self):
        """Increment the count by 1."""
        self.count += 1

    def get_count(self):
        """Return the current count."""
        return self.count

    def reset_count(self):
        """Reset the count to zero."""
        self.count = 0

# Example usage of the Counter class
counter = Counter()
print("Initial count:", counter.get_count())  # Output: 0

counter.increment()
counter.increment()
print("Count after two increments:", counter.get_count())  # Output: 2

counter.reset_count()
print("Count after reset:", counter.get_count())  # Output: 0

+       <#sep#>You are given the root of a binary tree. Your task is to write a function `tree_to_list(root)` that returns a list of lists, where each sublist contains the values of the nodes at a particular level of the tree, in left-to-right order. The root node is considered to be at level 0.

**Input:**
- The function takes a single argument, `root`, which is the root node of a binary tree. Each node in the tree is an instance of the `TreeNode` class defined as follows:
  ```python
  class TreeNode:
      def __init__(self, val=0, left=None, right=None):
          self.val = val
          self.left = left
          self.right = right
  ```

**Output:**
- The function should return a list of lists, where each sublist contains the values of the nodes at a particular level of the tree.

**Sample Input:**
```
    1
   / \
  2   3
 / \
4   5
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[[1], [2, 3], [4, 5]]
```

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, val=0, left=None, right=None):
        self.val = val
        self.left = left
        self.right = right

def tree_to_list(root):
    """
    Converts a binary tree into a list of lists, where each sublist represents a level of the tree.
    
    :param root: TreeNode, the root of the binary tree
    :return: List[List[int]], a list of lists containing the values of the nodes at each level
    """
    if not root:
        return []

    result = []
    queue = [root]

    while queue:
        level_size = len(queue)
        current_level = []

        for _ in range(level_size):
            node = queue.pop(0)
            current_level.append(node.val)

            if node.left:
                queue.append(node.left)
            if node.right:
                queue.append(node.right)

        result.append(current_level)

    return result
```<#sep#>, the problem reduces to:

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Matrix` class that supports basic matrix operations: addition, subtraction, and multiplication. The class should handle matrices of integers and ensure that operations are performed only on compatible matrices. If the matrices are not compatible for an operation, the method should raise a `ValueError` with an appropriate message.

**Input:**
- The `Matrix` class will be instantiated with a 2D list of integers representing the matrix.
- The `add`, `subtract`, and `multiply` methods will be called with another `Matrix` object as an argument.

**Output:**
- The `add` and `subtract` methods should return a new `Matrix` object representing the result of the operation.
- The `multiply` method should return a new `Matrix` object representing the product of the two matrices.
- The `__str__` method should return a string representation of the matrix, with rows separated by newlines and elements in each row separated by tabs.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
matrix1 = Matrix([[1, 2], [3, 4]])
matrix2 = Matrix([[2, 0], [1, 2]])
print(matrix1.add(matrix2))  # Output: 3	2
                             #         4	6
print(matrix1.subtract(matrix2))  # Output: -1	2
                                  #          2	2
print(matrix1.multiply(matrix2))  # Output: 4	4
                                  #          10	8
```

```python
class Matrix:
    def __init__(self, data):
        """
        Initialize a Matrix object with the given 2D list of integers.
        """
        self.data = data
        self.rows = len(data)
        self.cols = len(data[0]) if self.rows > 0 else 0

    def add(self, other):
        """
        Add this matrix to another matrix and return the result as a new Matrix object.
        Raises ValueError if the matrices are not of the same dimensions.
        """
        if self.rows != other.rows or self.cols != other.cols:
            raise ValueError("Matrices must have the same dimensions for addition.")
        
        result = [
            [self.data[i][j] + other.data[i][j] for j in range(self.cols)]
            for i in range(self.rows)
        ]
        return Matrix(result)

    def subtract(self, other):
        """
        Subtract another matrix from this matrix and return the result as a new Matrix object.
        Raises ValueError if the matrices are not of the same dimensions.
        """
        if self.rows != other.rows or self.cols != other.cols:
            raise ValueError("Matrices must have the same dimensions for subtraction.")
        
        result = [
            [self.data[i][j] - other.data[i][j] for j in range(self.cols)]
            for i in range(self.rows)
        ]
        return Matrix(result)

    def multiply(self, other):
        """
        Multiply this matrix by another matrix and return the result as a new Matrix object.
        Raises ValueError if the number of columns in this matrix is not equal to the number of rows in the other matrix.
        """
        if self.cols != other.rows:
            raise ValueError("Number of columns in the first matrix must be equal to number of rows in the second matrix.")
        
        result = [
            [sum(self.data[i][k] * other.data[k][j] for k in range(self.cols)) for j in range(other.cols)]
            for i in range(self.rows)
        ]
        return Matrix(result)

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Return a string representation of the matrix with rows separated by newlines
        and elements in each row separated by tabs.
        """
        return '\n'.join(['\t'.join(map(str, row)) for row in self.data])

<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that utilizes the `requests` library to retrieve the HTML content from a specified URL. Your program should output the HTTP status code and the length of the HTML content. If the request is unsuccessful, your program should output an error message. 

**Input:**
- A single string representing the URL.

**Output:**
- If the request is successful (status code 200), print two lines:
  - The first line should contain the status code.
  - The second line should contain the length of the HTML content.
- If the request fails, print an error message indicating the failure.

**Sample Input:**
```
https://www.example.com
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Status Code: 200
Length of the HTML content: 1234
```

**Sample Input:**
```
https://nonexistentwebsite.com
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Failed to retrieve the webpage. Status Code: 404
```

```python
import requests

def fetch_webpage(url):
    """
    Fetches the HTML content from the given URL and prints the status code and length of the content.
    If the request fails, prints an error message.

    :param url: The URL from which to fetch the HTML content.
    """
    try:
        response = requests.get(url)
        if response.status_code == 200:
            html_content = response.text
            print(f"Status Code: {response.status_code}")
            print(f"Length of the HTML content: {len(html_content)}")
        else:
            print(f"Failed to retrieve the webpage. Status Code: {response.status_code}")
    except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

# Example usage
url = 'https://www.example.com'
fetch_webpage(url)

**Final Answer**
\boxed{148}
</think>To determine the number of ways the board can look after Carl wins by placing his third 'O' on his sixth move, we need to consider the following steps:

1. **Identify Winning Lines for Carl**:
   There are 8 possible lines in a tic-tac-toe board: 3 rows, 3 columns, and 2 diagonals. Carl's three 'O's must form one of these lines, with the third 'O' placed on his sixth move.

2. **Azar's Moves**:
   Azar's three 'X's must not form a line. We need to ensure that the remaining cells (after placing Carl's three 'O's) do not form a line for Azar.

3. **Counting Valid Boards**:
   - For each winning line (row, column, or diagonal) that Carl could win with:
     - Calculate the number of ways Azar's 'X's can be placed in the remaining cells without forming a line.
     - For rows and columns, the remaining cells can form lines in two other rows or columns. Therefore, we subtract these invalid placements.
     - For diagonals, the remaining cells cannot form any line, so all placements are valid.

**Detailed Calculation**:
- **Rows and Columns**:
  Each row or column leaves 6 remaining cells. Azar's 'X's can form lines in two remaining rows or columns. The number of valid placements is:
  \[
  \binom{6}{3} - 2 = 20 - 2 = 18
  \]

- **Diagonals**:
  Each diagonal leaves 6 remaining cells where no lines can be formed. The number of valid placements is:
  \[
  \binom{6}{3} = 20
  \]

**Summing Up**:
- **3 rows**: \( 3 \times 18 = 54 \)
- **3 columns**: \( 3 \times 18 = 54 \)
- **2 diagonals**: \( 2 \times 20 = 40 \)

Total number of valid boards:
\[
54 + 54 + 40 = 148
\]

Thus, the number of ways the board can look after the game is over is \(\boxed{148}\).<#sep#> is_boundary = True.

<#sep#>You are given a list of integers `values`. Your task is to construct a binary search tree (BST) from these integers. Each integer should be inserted into the BST following the standard BST rules: for any given node, all values in the left subtree must be less than the node's value, and all values in the right subtree must be greater than the node's value. Implement a function `generate_binary_tree` that takes the list `values` and returns the root of the constructed BST. Each node in the tree should be an instance of the `TreeNode` class, which has attributes `val`, `left`, and `right`.

**Input:**
- A list of integers `values` (1 ≤ len(values) ≤ 10^3), where each integer is in the range [-10^6, 10^6].

**Output:**
- The root of the constructed binary search tree.

**Sample Input:**
```
[3, 1, 4, 2, 5]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
    3
   / \
  1   4
   \   \
    2   5
```

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, val=0, left=None, right=None):
        self.val = val
        self.left = left
        self.right = right

def insert_into_bst(root, val):
    """
    Inserts a value into the BST rooted at 'root'.
    
    :param root: TreeNode, the root of the BST.
    :param val: int, the value to be inserted.
    :return: TreeNode, the root of the BST after insertion.
    """
    if not root:
        return TreeNode(val)
    if val < root.val:
        root.left = insert_into_bst(root.left, val)
    else:
        root.right = insert_into_bst(root.right, val)
    return root

def generate_binary_tree(values):
    """
    Constructs a binary search tree from a list of integers.
    
    :param values: List[int], the list of integers to be inserted into the BST.
    :return: TreeNode, the root of the constructed BST.
    """
    if not values:
        return None
    root = TreeNode(values[0])
    for value in values[1:]:
        insert_into_bst(root, value)
    return root

# Example usage:
values = [3, 1, 4, 2, 5]
root = generate_binary_tree(values)

### What's the problem?

With DynamoDB+boto3, when a transaction fails due to a `ConditionExpression` failing,
AND we've set `"ReturnValuesOnConditionCheckFailure": "ALL_OLD",` we should see the
current item in the error response.

For example, if we ran `order-breakfast` when hitting DynamoDB for real, here's the output
of catching the exception and pprinting `err.reason`:

```python
{'CancellationReasons': [{'Code': 'ConditionalCheckFailed',
                          'Item': {'customer': {'S': 'mark'},
                                   'lock': {'M': {'acquired_at': {'N': '123'}}},

                          'Message': 'The conditional request failed'}],
 'Error': {'Code': 'TransactionCanceledException',
           'Message': 'Transaction cancelled, please refer cancellation '
                      'reasons for specific reasons [ConditionalCheckFailed]'},

            'specific reasons [ConditionalCheckFailed]',

<#sep#>You are given two non-empty strings `str1` and `str2`. Your task is to implement a function `find_common_prefix` that determines the longest common prefix shared by both strings. If no common prefix exists, return an empty string.

**Input:**
- Two strings `str1` and `str2` (1 ≤ |str1|, |str2| ≤ 10^5), consisting of lowercase English letters.

**Output:**
- A single string representing the longest common prefix of `str1` and `str2`. If no common prefix exists, return an empty string.

**Sample Input:**
```
flower
flow
```

**Sample Output:**
```
flow
```

```python
def find_common_prefix(str1, str2):
    """
    Determines the longest common prefix shared by two strings.

    Parameters:
    str1 (str): The first input string.
    str2 (str): The second input string.

    Returns:
    str: The longest common prefix of str1 and str2. Returns an empty string if no common prefix exists.
    """
    common_prefix = ""
    min_length = min(len(str1), len(str2))
    
    for i in range(min_length):
        if str1[i] == str2[i]:
            common_prefix += str1[i]
        else:
            break
    
    return common_prefix

# Example usage
print(find_common_prefix("flower", "flow"))  # Output: "flow"

Then, reverse the digits, and take the difference of these two numbers. Reverse the digits once more, and add these two numbers together. You will get \$ 1089 \$. The magic is left as an exercise to the reader.
Taking this a step further, let's define an algorithm for all possible integers:

Choose any positive integer \$ n \$
Reverse the digits of \$ n \$, and call it \$ x \$
Set \$ n \$ equal to \$ n - x \$ if \$ x < n \$, and \$ n + x \$ otherwise
Repeat the above step until \$ n \$ reaches a number it has already visited

In turns out that this algorithm gets stuck in an infinite loop, for certain numbers. Let's call a number 1089-able™, if it does in fact terminate (in other words, a duplicate is reached at some point). For example, everyone's favorite number, 2022, is 1089-able™:

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python generator function named `fibonacci` that generates the Fibonacci sequence indefinitely. The Fibonacci sequence starts with 0 and 1, and each subsequent number is the sum of the two preceding ones. Your function should yield the first number in the sequence (0), followed by the second number (1), and continue to yield the sum of the two preceding numbers indefinitely.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- The function does not take any input parameters.
- The function yields an infinite sequence of Fibonacci numbers starting from 0.

```python
def fibonacci():
    """
    A generator function that yields the Fibonacci sequence indefinitely.
    Yields:
        int: The next number in the Fibonacci sequence.
    """
    a, b = 0, 1
    while True:
        yield a
        a, b = b, a + b

--- a/moto/ses/template.py
+++ b/moto/ses/template.py
@@ -1,53 +1,180 @@
-from typing import Any, Dict, Optional
+from typing import Any, Dict, Optional, Type
 
 from .exceptions import MissingRenderingAttributeException
 from moto.utilities.tokenizer import GenericTokenizer
 
 
+class BlockProcessor:
+    def __init__(
+        self, template: str, template_data: Dict[str, Any], tokenizer: GenericTokenizer
+    ):
+        self.template = template
+        self.template_data = template_data
+        self.tokenizer = tokenizer
+
+    def parse(self) -> str:
+        # Added to make MyPy happy
+        # Not all implementations have this method
+        # It's up to the caller to know whether to call this method
+        raise NotImplementedError
+
+
+class EachBlockProcessor(BlockProcessor):
+    def __init__(
+        self, template: str, template_data: Dict[str, Any], tokenizer: GenericTokenizer
+    ):
+        self.template = template
+        self.tokenizer = tokenizer
+
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("#each")
+        self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+        var_name = self.tokenizer.read_until("}}").strip()
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("}}")
+        self.template_data = template_data.get(var_name, [])
+
+    def parse(self) -> str:
+        parsed = ""
+        current_pos = self.tokenizer.token_pos
+
+        for template_data in self.template_data:
+            self.tokenizer.token_pos = current_pos
+            for char in self.tokenizer:
+                if char == "{" and self.tokenizer.peek() == "{":
+                    self.tokenizer.skip_characters("{")
+                    self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+
+                    _processor = get_processor(self.tokenizer)(
+                        self.template, template_data, self.tokenizer  # type: ignore
+                    )
+                    # If we've reached the end, we should stop processing
+                    # Our parent will continue with whatever comes after {{/each}}
+                    if type(_processor) == EachEndBlockProcessor:
+                        break
+                    # If we've encountered another processor, they can continue
+                    parsed += _processor.parse()
+
+                    continue
+
+                parsed += char
+
+        return parsed
+
+
+class EachEndBlockProcessor(BlockProcessor):
+    def __init__(
+        self, template: str, template_data: Dict[str, Any], tokenizer: GenericTokenizer
+    ):
+        super().__init__(template, template_data, tokenizer)
+
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("/each")
+        self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("}}")
+
+
+class IfBlockProcessor(BlockProcessor):
+    def __init__(
+        self, template: str, template_data: Dict[str, Any], tokenizer: GenericTokenizer
+    ):
+        super().__init__(template, template_data, tokenizer)
+
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("#if")
+        self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+        condition = self.tokenizer.read_until("}}").strip()
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("}}")
+        self.parse_contents = template_data.get(condition)
+
+    def parse(self) -> str:
+        parsed = ""
+
+        for char in self.tokenizer:
+            if char == "{" and self.tokenizer.peek() == "{":
+                self.tokenizer.skip_characters("{")
+                self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+
+                _processor = get_processor(self.tokenizer)(
+                    self.template, self.template_data, self.tokenizer
+                )
+                if type(_processor) == IfEndBlockProcessor:
+                    break
+                elif type(_processor) == ElseBlockProcessor:
+                    self.parse_contents = not self.parse_contents
+                    continue
+                if self.parse_contents:
+                    parsed += _processor.parse()
+
+                continue
+
+            if self.parse_contents:
+                parsed += char
+
+        return parsed
+
+
+class IfEndBlockProcessor(BlockProcessor):
+    def __init__(
+        self, template: str, template_data: Dict[str, Any], tokenizer: GenericTokenizer
+    ):
+        super().__init__(template, template_data, tokenizer)
+
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("/if")
+        self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("}}")
+
+
+class ElseBlockProcessor(BlockProcessor):
+    def __init__(
+        self, template: str, template_data: Dict[str, Any], tokenizer: GenericTokenizer
+    ):
+        super().__init__(template, template_data, tokenizer)
+
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("else")
+        self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("}}")
+
+
+class VarBlockProcessor(BlockProcessor):
+    def parse(self) -> str:
+        var_name = self.tokenizer.read_until("}}").strip()
+        if self.template_data.get(var_name) is None:
+            raise MissingRenderingAttributeException(var_name)
+        data: str = self.template_data.get(var_name)  # type: ignore
+        self.tokenizer.skip_white_space()
+        self.tokenizer.skip_characters("}}")
+        return data
+
+
+def get_processor(tokenizer: GenericTokenizer) -> Type[BlockProcessor]:
+    if tokenizer.peek(5) == "#each":
+        return EachBlockProcessor
+    if tokenizer.peek(5) == "/each":
+        return EachEndBlockProcessor
+    if tokenizer.peek(3) == "#if":
+        return IfBlockProcessor
+    if tokenizer.peek(3) == "/if":
+        return IfEndBlockProcessor
+    if tokenizer.peek(4) == "else":
+        return ElseBlockProcessor
+    return VarBlockProcessor
+
+
 def parse_template(

Your task is to write a Python function `sum_numbers_in_file` that reads this file and returns the sum of all the numbers. Your function should handle any `IOError` exceptions that might occur during file reading and return `None` in such cases.

**Sample Input:**
```
1.5
2.3
3.7
```

**Sample Output:**
```
7.5
```

```python
def sum_numbers_in_file(filename):
    """
    Reads a file containing numbers, one per line, and returns the sum of these numbers.
    Handles IOError exceptions and returns None if an error occurs.

    :param filename: str, the name of the file to read
    :return: float, the sum of the numbers in the file or None if an error occurs
    """
    try:
        with open(filename, 'r') as file:
            total_sum = sum(float(line.strip()) for line in file)
        return total_sum
    except IOError:
        return None

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing an optimized version of the bubble sort algorithm in Python. Your function should be able to sort an array of integers in ascending order. To optimize the algorithm, include a mechanism to detect if the array is already sorted and terminate early if no swaps are made during a pass through the array.

**Input:**
- A list of integers `arr` (1 ≤ len(arr) ≤ 1000), where each integer is in the range -1000 ≤ arr[i] ≤ 1000.

**Output:**
- Return the sorted list of integers.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[64, 34, 25, 12, 22, 11, 90]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[11, 12, 22, 25, 34, 64, 90]
```

```python
def optimize_bubble_sort(arr):
    """
    Sorts a list of integers in ascending order using an optimized version of the bubble sort algorithm.
    
    Parameters:
    arr (list): A list of integers to be sorted.
    
    Returns:
    list: The sorted list of integers.
    """
    n = len(arr)
    for i in range(n):
        swapped = False
        for j in range(0, n - i - 1):
            if arr[j] > arr[j + 1]:
                arr[j], arr[j + 1] = arr[j + 1], arr[j]
                swapped = True
        if not swapped:
            break
    return arr

</think>To evaluate the integral \(\int \frac{1 + x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \, dx\), we start by splitting the integrand into two parts:

\[
\int \frac{1 + x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \, dx = \int \left( \frac{1}{1 + x e^x} + \frac{x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \right) \, dx.
\]

First, consider the second term \(\frac{x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x}\). We can rewrite it as:

\[
\frac{x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} = \frac{x e^x \cdot e^x}{1 + x e^x} = \frac{x e^x \cdot e^x}{1 + x e^x} = \frac{e^x \cdot x e^x}{1 + x e^x}.
\]

Let \(u = 1 + x e^x\). Then, \(du = (e^x + x e^x) \, dx = e^x (1 + x) \, dx\). Therefore, we have:

\[
\frac{e^x \cdot x e^x}{1 + x e^x} \, dx = \frac{e^x \cdot (u - 1)}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)} = \frac{u - 1}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{1 + x}.
\]

However, we need to express \(dx\) in terms of \(du\):

\[
dx = \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)}.
\]

Substituting back, we get:

\[
\int \frac{x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \, dx = \int \frac{u - 1}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{1 + x} = \int \frac{u - 1}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{1 + x}.
\]

Now, we can split the integral:

\[
\int \frac{1 + x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \, dx = \int \frac{1}{1 + x e^x} \, dx + \int \frac{x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \, dx.
\]

We simplify the integrand using polynomial division:

\[
1 + x e^{2x} = (1 + x e^x) e^x + (1 - e^x).
\]

Thus, the integrand becomes:

\[
\frac{1 + x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} = e^x + \frac{1 - e^x}{1 + x e^x}.
\]

So, the integral can be rewritten as:

\[
\int \left( e^x + \frac{1 - e^x}{1 + x e^x} \right) \, dx = \int e^x \, dx + \int \frac{1 - e^x}{1 + x e^x} \, dx.
\]

The first integral is straightforward:

\[
\int e^x \, dx = e^x + C_1.
\]

For the second integral, let \(u = 1 + x e^x\<#sep#> D's next step (when trying to go to B again).

<#sep#>You are given an integer `num`. Your task is to determine whether `num` is a palindrome. A number is a palindrome if it reads the same backward as forward. For instance, 121 is a palindrome, whereas 123 is not. Note that negative numbers are not considered palindromes. Implement the function `is_palindrome_number(num)` that returns `True` if `num` is a palindrome and `False` otherwise. The function should handle zero correctly.

**Sample Input and Output:**
- Input: `121`
  - Output: `True`
- Input: `-121`
  - Output: `False`
- Input: `10`
  - Output: `False`
- Input: `0`
  - Output: `True`

```python
def is_palindrome_number(num):
    """
    Determines if the given integer is a palindrome.
    
    Args:
    num (int): The integer to check.
    
    Returns:
    bool: True if num is a palindrome, False otherwise.
    """
    # Convert the number to a string
    num_str = str(num)
    # Check if the string is equal to its reverse
    return num_str == num_str[::-1]

# Example usage:
# print(is_palindrome_number(121))  # True
# print(is_palindrome_number(-121)) # False
# print(is_palindrome_number(10))   # False
# print(is_palindrome_number(0))    # True
```<#sep#> + x e^x}.
\]

So, the integral can be rewritten as:

\[
\int \left( e^x + \frac{1 - e^x}{1 + x e^x} \right) \, dx = \int e^x \, dx + \int \frac{1 - e^x}{1 + x e^x} \, dx.
\]

The first integral is straightforward:

\[
\int e^x \, dx = e^x + C_1.
\]

For the second integral, let \(u = 1 + x e^x\). Then, \(du = (e^x + x e^x) \, dx = e^x (1 + x) \, dx\). Therefore, we have:

\[
\int \frac{1 - e^x}{1 + x e^x} \, dx = \int \frac{1 - e^x}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)}.
\]

Simplifying the integrand:

\[
\int \frac{1 - e^x}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)} = \int \frac{1 - e^x}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)} = \int \frac{1 - e^x}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)}.
\]

We can split the integral:

\[
\int \frac{1 - e^x}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)} = \int \frac{1}{u} \, du - \int \frac{e^x}{u} \cdot \frac{du}{e^x (1 + x)} = \int \frac{1}{u} \, du - \int \frac{1}{u} \, du = -\ln|u| + C_2.
\]

Substituting back \(u = 1 + x e^x\):

\[
-\ln|1 + x e^x| + C_2.
\]

Combining the results, we get:

\[
\int \frac{1 + x e^{2x}}{1 + x e^x} \, dx = e^x - \ln|1 + x e^x| + C.
\]

Therefore, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{e^x + x - \ln|1 + x e^x| + C}.
\]<#sep#>611,071 = 781,187 → n = 781187.

                                    "Value": f"192.0.2.{ip}",
                                },
                            ],
                            "TTL": 60,
                            "Type": "A",
                        },
                    },
                ],
                "Comment": "Web server for example.com",
            },
            HostedZoneId=hosted_zone_id,
        )

                HostedZoneId=hosted_zone["Id"],
        )

    instances = records["ResourceRecordSets"]
    assert len(instances) == 300

Your task is to implement a function `find_common_words` that returns a set of words that appear in both strings, ignoring case differences. Assume that the input strings are properly formatted and do not contain any punctuation marks.

**Input:**
- Two strings `s1` and `s2` (1 ≤ |s1|, |s2| ≤ 1000).

**Output:**
- A set of words that are common to both strings, with all words in lowercase.

**Sample Input:**
```
s1 = "Hello world this is a test"
s2 = "This test is simple"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
{'this', 'is', 'test'}
```

```python
def find_common_words(s1, s2):
    """
    Returns a set of words that appear in both input strings, ignoring case differences.
    
    :param s1: First input string
    :param s2: Second input string
    :return: Set of common words in lowercase
    """
    s1 = s1.lower()
    s2 = s2.lower()
    
    words1 = set(s1.split())
    words2 = set(s2.split())
    
    return words1.intersection(words2)

# Example usage:
s1 = "Hello world this is a test"
s2 = "This test is simple"
print(find_common_words(s1, s2))
```<#sep#>Solve for \(a, b \in \mathbb{Z}\) such that \(\frac{2}{a} = \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{6}\).

<think>
Okay, let's see. I need to find integers a and b such that 2/a equals 1/b plus 1/6. Hmm, so the equation is 2/a = 1/b + 1/6. Both a and b are integers, right? Alright, let me start by trying to rearrange the equation to express one variable in terms of the other. Maybe I can solve for one variable first.

Let me subtract 1/b from both sides to get 2/a - 1/b = 1/6. Hmm, but that might not be the easiest way. Alternatively, I can combine the terms on the right side. Let me try to get a common denominator for 1/b and 1/6. The common denominator would be 6b. So, 1/b is 6/(6b) and 1/6 is b/(6b). So adding them together gives (6 + b)/(6b). Therefore, the equation becomes 2/a = (6 + b)/(6b).

Then, cross-multiplying both sides to eliminate the fractions: 2 * 6b = a * (6 + b). Simplifying the left side: 12b = a(6 + b). So, 12b = a(b + 6). I need to find integers a and b such that this holds.

Let me rearrange this equation to solve for a. So, a = 12b / (b + 6). Since a and b are integers, the denominator (b + 6) must divide 12b exactly. Therefore, (b + 6) divides 12b. Let's write this as (b + 6) | 12b. Which implies that (b + 6) divides 12b.

Another way to think about this is that (b + 6) divides 12b. Let me recall that if d divides n, then d divides n mod d. So, perhaps I can express this as (b + 6) divides 12b - 12*(b + 6) + 12*(b + 6). Wait, maybe that's complicating. Alternatively, maybe note that since (b + 6) divides 12b, then (b + 6) must also divide 12b - 12*(b + 6). Let me calculate that:

+  <IsTruncated<#sep#>You are given a list of integers `numbers`. Your task is to implement a function `find_max` that returns the maximum integer in the list. If the list is empty, the function should return `None`. Your implementation should handle any edge cases appropriately.

**Sample Input:**
```python
numbers = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6, 5, 3, 5]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
9
```

**Constraints:**
- The list `numbers` can contain between 0 and 10^5 integers.
- Each integer in the list can range from -10^9 to 10^9.

```python
def find_max(numbers):
    """
    Returns the maximum integer in the list `numbers`.
    If the list is empty, returns `None`.
    
    :param numbers: List of integers
    :return: Maximum integer in the list or None if the list is empty
    """
    if not numbers:
        return None
    
    max_num = numbers[0]
    
    for i in numbers:
        if i > max_num:
            max_num = i
    
    return max_num

</think>To solve the equation \(\frac{2}{a} = \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{6}\) for integers \(a\) and \(b\), we start by rewriting the equation:

\[
\frac{2}{a} = \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{6}
\]

First, we combine the terms on the right-hand side by finding a common denominator:

\[
\frac{2}{a} = \frac{6 + b}{6b}
\]

Next, we cross-multiply to eliminate the fractions:

\[
2 \cdot 6b = a \cdot (6 + b)
\]

Simplifying the left-hand side:

\[
12b = a(6 + b)
\]

Rearranging to solve for \(a\):

\[
a = \frac{12b}{6 + b}
\]

For \(a\) to be an integer, the denominator \(6 + b\) must divide \(12b\) exactly. Therefore, we need \(6 + b\) to be a divisor of 12b. We can express this as:

\[
6 + b \mid 12b
\]

Using the property of divisors, we know that if \(6 + b\) divides \(12b\), then it must also divide \(12b - 12(6 + b)\):

\[
12b - 12(6 + b) = 12b - 72 - 12b = -72
\]

Thus, \(6 + b\) must be a divisor of 72. The divisors of 72 are:

\[
\pm 1, \pm 2, \pm 3, \pm 4, \pm 6, \pm 8, \pm 9, \pm 12, \pm 18, \pm 24, \pm 36, \pm 72
\]

We set \(6 + b = d\), where \(d\) is a divisor of 72. Then:

\[
b = d - 6
\]

Substituting \(b = d - 6\) into the equation for \(a\):

\[
a = \frac{12(d - 6)}{d} = 12 - \frac{72}{d}
\]

For \(a\) to be an integer, \(\frac{72}{d}\) must be an integer. Therefore, \(d\) must be a divisor of 72. We now list all divisors \(d\) and compute \(a\) and \(b\):

1. \(d = 1\):
   \[
   b = 1 - 6 = -5, \quad a = 12 - \frac{72}{1} = 12 - 72 = -60
   \]
   Solution: \((-60, -5)\)

2. \(d = 2\):
   \[
   b = 2 - 6 = -4, \quad a = 12 - \frac{72}{2} = 12 - 36 = -24
   \]
   Solution: \((-24, -4)\)

3. \(d = 3\):
   \[
   b = 3 - 6 = -3, \quad a = 12 - \frac{72}{3} = 12 - 24 = -12
   \]
   Solution: \((-12, -3)\)

<#sep#>You are given a list of integers. Your task is to write a function that returns a new list containing only the unique elements from the input list, preserving the order of their first appearance. 

**Input:**
- A list of integers `input_list` (1 ≤ len(input_list) ≤ 10^5), where each integer is in the range [-10^9, 10^9].

**Output:**
- A list of integers representing the unique elements from `input_list` in the order they first appeared.

**Sample Input:**
```
[4, 5, 6, 7, 5, 4, 8, 9, 10]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10]
```

```python
def remove_duplicates(input_list):
    """
    Returns a list of unique elements from the input list, preserving the order of their first appearance.
    
    :param input_list: List of integers
    :return: List of unique integers
    """
    seen = set()
    unique_list = []
    for item in input_list:
        if item not in seen:
            unique_list.append(item)
            seen.add(item)
    return unique_list

# Example usage:
input_list = [4, 5, 6, 7, 5, 4, 8, 9, 10]
print(remove_duplicates(input_list))  # Output: [4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10]

+        self.create<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Car` with the following specifications:

- **Attributes:**
  - `make`: A string representing the manufacturer of the car.
  - `model`: A string representing the model of the car.
  - `year`: An integer representing the year the car was manufactured.
  - `mileage`: An integer representing the current mileage of the car.

- **Methods:**
  - `get_make()`: Returns the make of the car.
  - `get_model()`: Returns the model of the car.
  - `get_year()`: Returns the year of the car.
  - `get_mileage()`: Returns the mileage of the car.
  - `set_make(make)`: Sets the make of the car.
  - `set_model(model)`: Sets the model of the car.
  - `set_year(year)`: Sets the year of the car.
  - `set_mileage(mileage)`: Sets the mileage of the car.
  - `update_mileage(new_mileage)`: Updates the mileage of the car. Raises a `ValueError` if `new_mileage` is less than the current mileage.
  - `display_info()`: Prints the car's information in the format:
    ```
    Car Information:
    Make: <make>
    Model: <model>
    Year: <year>
    Mileage: <mileage> miles
    ```

- **Sample Input/Output:**
  - Input: `Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2021, 15000)`
  - Output:
    ```
    Car Information:
    Make: Toyota
    Model: Corolla
    Year: 2021
    Mileage: 15000 miles
    ```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, mileage):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the given make, model, year, and mileage.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.mileage = mileage

    def get_make(self):
        """
        Returns the make of the car.
        """
        return self.make

    def get_model(self):
        """
        Returns the model of the car.
        """
        return self.model

    def get_year(self):
        """
        Returns the year of the car.
        """
        return self.year

    def get_mileage(self):
        """
        Returns the mileage of the car.
        """
        return self.mileage

    def set_make(self, make):
        """
        Sets the make of the car.
        """
        self.make = make

    def set_model(self, model):
        """
        Sets the model of the car.
        """
        self.model = model

    def set_year(self, year):
        """
        Sets the year of the car.
        """
        self.year = year

    def set_mileage(self, mileage):
        """
        Sets the mileage of the car.
        """
        self.mileage = mileage

    def update_mileage(self, new_mileage):
        """
        Updates the mileage of the car. Raises a ValueError if new_mileage is less than the current mileage.
        """
        if new_mileage < self.mileage:
            raise ValueError("New mileage cannot be less than the current mileage.")
        self.mileage = new_mileage

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the car's information in a readable format.
        """
        print(f"Car Information:\nMake: {self.make}\nModel: {self.model}\nYear: {self.year}\nMileage: {self.mileage} miles")

# Creating an instance of the Car class
my_car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2021, 15000)

# Displaying the car's information
my_car.display_info()
```<#sep#>What is the largest whole number that cannot be expressed as \(6a + 9b + 20c\), where \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) are non-negative integers?

<think>
Okay, so I need to find the largest whole number that can't be written as 6a + 9b + 20c, where a, b, c are non-negative integers. Hmm, this seems like a problem related to the Frobenius number, which is the largest number that can't be expressed as a linear combination of given numbers with non-negative coefficients. But I remember that the Frobenius number is only straightforward to compute for two numbers, right? For three or more numbers, there's no general formula. So maybe I need another approach here.

First, let me recall that for two numbers, say x and y, the Frobenius number is xy - x - y if they are coprime. But here, we have three numbers: 6, 9, and 20. Let me check if these numbers have a common divisor. The gcd of 6 and 9 is 3, and 20 and 3 are coprime. So the gcd of all three is 1? Wait, 6 and 9 have gcd 3, and gcd(3, 20) is 1. So the overall gcd is 1. That means, according to the Chicken McNugget Theorem, there should be a largest number beyond which all numbers can be expressed as such a combination. But since there are three numbers, it's more complicated.

I think the way to approach this is to check consecutive numbers and see which ones can be expressed in the form 6a + 9b + 20c. Once we find a consecutive run of numbers (as many as the smallest coefficient, which is 6 here) that can all be expressed, then every number beyond that can also be expressed by adding multiples of 6. So the largest number that can't be expressed would be just before that run.

Let me start by listing out numbers and see if they can be formed.

Starting from 1 upwards:

1: Can't be formed, since the smallest number we can make is 6.

2: Same as 1.

3: Let's see, 6, 9, 20 are all larger. Wait, but 3 is a divisor of 6 and 9. But 3 itself can't be formed unless one of the coefficients allows. But 6a would give multiples of 3, as 9b is also multiples of 3, and 20c is 20 which is not a multiple of 3. So 20c mod 3 is 2c. So to get 3, we need the total sum to be 3. So:

3 = 6a + 9b + 20c.

Since 6a and 9b are multiples of 3, and 20c is 20c = 3*6 + 2c, so 20c ≡ 2c mod 3. So overall, the equation modulo 3 would be:

0 ≡ 0 + 0 + 2c mod 3.

So 2c ≡ 0 mod 3 ⇒ c ≡ 0 mod 3. So c must be 0, 3, 6,... But since 20c has to be ≤ 3, which is impossible. Therefore, 3 cannot be expressed.

Similarly, 4: Let's check.

4: 6a + 9b + 20c. Similarly, mod 3:

4 ≡ 1 mod 3. So 1 ≡ 0 + 0 + 2c mod 3 ⇒ 2c ≡ 1 mod 3 ⇒ c ≡ 2 mod 3. So c must be 2, 5, 8,... But 20*2=40 which is way larger than 4. So 4 can't be formed either.

5: Same approach. 5 mod 3 is 2.

So 2 ≡ 2c mod 3 ⇒ c ≡ 1 mod 3. c=1,4,7,... 20*1=20 >5. So no.

6: 6*1 + 0 + 0 = 6. So yes. 6 is expressible.

7: Let's check. Trying combinations. Since 6 is a term, 7 could be 6 + 1, but 1 isn't a coefficient here. Alternatively, maybe 20c is involved? 20c would need to be 7-6a-9b. But 20c would be too big. So 7: maybe not.

Alternatively, 7 mod 3 is 1. So 2c ≡1 mod 3. So c≡2 mod3. c=2: 40. 40>7. So no. So 7 can't be formed.

8: 8 mod 3 is 2. So 2c ≡2 mod3 ⇒ c≡1 mod3. So c=1:20. 20>8. So no.

9: 0*6 +1*9 +0*20 =9. So yes.

10: Let's try. 10 = 6a +9b +20c. Let me check possible c values. c=0: Then 10=6a +9b. Let's see possible a and b. 10 is not a multiple of 3, but 6a +9b is 3(2a + 3b). So 10 is not a multiple of 3, so no solution here. c=0.5: Not allowed. c=1: 20*1=20>10. So no. Thus, 10 can't be formed.

11: Similarly, 11 mod3=2. 2c≡2 mod3 ⇒c=1 mod3. So c=1:20>11. No. c=0: 11=6a +9b. 11 is not a multiple of3. So no. 11 can't be formed.

12: 6*2=12. Yes.

13: Let's see. 13 mod3=1. So 2c≡1 mod3 ⇒ c=2 mod3. c=2:20*2=40>13. So no. c=0:13=6a +9b. 13 isn't a multiple of3. c=1:20. So 13-20= -7. Negative, so no. So 13 can't be formed.

14: 14 mod3=2. 2c≡2 mod3⇒c=1 mod3. c=1:20. 14-20= -6. So negative. c=0:14=6a +9b. Let's check: 14/6≈2.333. a=2:6*2=12, 14-12=2. Not 9b. a=1:6*1=6, 14-6=8, not 9b. a=0:14=9b. Not possible. So 14 can't be formed.

15: 9*1 +6*1=15. Yes: 6+9=15. Or 9*1 +6*1 +20*0=15. So yes.

16: Let's see. 16=6a +9b +20c. Trying c=0: 16=6a +9b. 16 is not a multiple of3. c=1:20. 16-20= -4. So no. c=0.5:invalid. So maybe c=0. So 16 can't be formed with c=0. Hmm. Wait, 16 mod3=1. So 2c≡1 mod3⇒c=2 mod3. So c=2:40, 40>16. No. So 16 can't be formed.

17: 17 mod3=2. 2c≡2 mod3⇒c=1 mod3. c=1:20. 17-20=-3. So no. c=0:17=6a +9b. 17 isn't multiple of3. So no.

18: 9*2=18. Yes.

19: 19 mod3=1. 2c≡1 mod3⇒c=2 mod3. c=2:40>19. c=0:19 not multiple of3. So no.

20: 20*1=20. Yes.

Thus, the code in Python would be:

```python
n=int(input())
v={n}
for _ in[0]*1<<16:
    r=int(str(n)[::-1])
    n =r<n and n-r or n+r
    if n in v:exit(print(0))
    v.add(n)
exit(1)
```

 This code runs for up to 2^16 steps (a large number but may suffice for most test cases). 

 If the process loops within that count, returns 0 (meaning it terminates hence able). If exceeds, returns 1 indicating non-able. 

 But the problem requires to return whether it terminates—the code's approach would say that returns 0 only if detected repeat before, else assumes not able. Thus, the output for 10057 would print 1, indicating non_able.

 This is the best approach I can come up with for code golf. 

调整 the loop count may be,necessary, but with flexibility, this would work. 

**Final Answer**
The shortest way is to implementing the simulation with a step limit. Thus the code would be:

Hence the Python code:
```python
n=int(input())
v={n}
for _ in[0]*~(0b1<<17):
    r=int(str(n)[::-1])
    n += r*(n<r)-(-r)
    if n in v:exit(print(0))
    v.add(n)
print(1)
```

- **Initialization**: The constructor should accept an optional `initial_balance` parameter, defaulting to 0 if not provided.
- **Deposit**: A method `deposit` that takes an `amount` parameter and adds it to the account balance. If the `amount` is not positive, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Deposit amount must be positive."
- **Withdraw**: A method `withdraw` that takes an `amount` parameter and subtracts it from the account balance. If the `amount` is greater than the current balance, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Insufficient funds." If the `amount` is not positive, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Withdrawal amount must be positive."
- **Get Balance**: A method `get_balance` that returns the current balance of the account.
- **Apply Interest**: A method `apply_interest` that takes an `interest_rate` parameter (as a percentage) and updates the balance by applying the interest. If the `interest_rate` is negative, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Interest rate must be non-negative."

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account = BankAccount(100)
account.deposit(50)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 150
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 120
account.apply_interest(10)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 132.0
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initializes a new bank account with an optional initial balance.
        
        :param initial_balance: The starting balance of the account (default is 0).
        """
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Adds the specified amount to the account balance.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
    
    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Subtracts the specified amount from the account balance.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the amount is greater than the current balance or not positive.
        """
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds.")
        elif amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        else:
            self.balance -= amount
    
    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: The current balance.
        """
        return self.balance
    
    def apply_interest(self, interest_rate):
        """
        Applies the specified interest rate to the account balance.
        
        :param interest_rate: The interest rate as a percentage.
        :raises ValueError: If the interest rate is negative.
        """
        if interest_rate < 0:
            raise ValueError("Interest rate must be non-negative.")
        self.balance += self.balance * (interest_rate / 100)

18: 9*2=18. Yes.

19: 19 mod3=1. 2c≡1 mod3⇒c=2 mod3. c=2:40>19. c=0:19 not multiple of3. So no.

20: 20*1=20. Yes.

The University of Aizu Elementary School (Aizu University and Small) also had a plan for a school trip next year. It is a tradition to travel by train on school trips. As the director of the Aizu University and Small Competitive Programming Department, you are looking forward to your school trip. If you want to make your school trip a success, you have made suggestions to the teachers. "Act in each class and avoid arriving at the same station at the same time !! It's easy if you apply your knowledge of competitive programming !!"

When traveling so that multiple classes do not arrive at the same station at the same time (multiple classes can stay at the same station), find the maximum number of classes that can reach the destination from the departure station. Create a program that seeks the minimum fare for the time. However, if there are trains that arrive at a certain station and trains that depart at the same time, it is possible to make a connection. In other words, the time required for transit can be ignored.
Input

The input consists of multiple datasets. The number of datasets is 20 or less. The format of each data set is as follows.


n
m1
x1,1 y1,1 c1,1
x1,2 y1,2 c1,2
...
x1, m1 y1, m1 c1, m1
m2
x2,1 y2,1 c2,1
x2,2 y2,2 c2,2
...
x2, m2 y2, m2 c2, m2
...
mn-1
xn-1,1 yn-1,1 cn-1,1
xn-1,2 yn-1,2 cn-1,2
...
xn-1, m2 yn-1, m2 cn-1, m2
g


n (2 ≤ n ≤ 100) represents the number of stations. The number of stations includes departure stations, destination stations, and transfer stations. The station that departs is counted as the first station, the station that transfers first is counted as the second station, and the destination is the nth station. mi represents the number of trains from the i-th station to the i + 1-th station. Information on mi (1 ≤ m ≤ 20) trains follows. xi, j yi, j ci, j represent the departure time (xi, j), arrival time (yi, j) and fare (ci, j) of the train from the i-th station to the i + 1th station. (0 ≤ xi, j, yi, j ≤ 200000 and xi, j ≤ yi, j) (1 ≤ ci, j ≤ 1000) followed by g (1 ≤ g ≤ 10). g represents the number of classes participating in the school trip. The numbers given (n, m, x, y, c, g) are all integers. The end of input is indicated by one line containing one 0.

Output

Output the number of classes that can arrive in one line and the minimum fare at that time in this order. If even one class cannot arrive, output 0 0.

Example

Input

2
4
1 2 10
2 2 5
1 3 20
2 3 10
10
3
2
1 2 5
0 3 4
3
10 11 100
2 12 2
12 12 3
10
0


Output

2 111

<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Student` with the following specifications:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the student's name.
  - `age`: An integer representing the student's age.
  - `student_id`: A string representing the student's unique identifier.
  - `grades`: A list of integers representing the student's grades.

- **Methods:**
  - `get_name()`: Returns the student's name.
  - `set_name(name)`: Sets the student's name.
  - `get_age()`: Returns the student's age.
  - `set_age(age)`: Sets the student's age.
  - `get_student_id()`: Returns the student's ID.
  - `set_student_id(student_id)`: Sets the student's ID.
  - `get_grades()`: Returns the list of grades.
  - `set_grades(grades)`: Sets the list of grades.
  - `calculate_average()`: Computes and returns the average of the grades. If the list is empty, return 0.
  - `display_info()`: Prints the student's information in the following format:
    ```
    Student Name: [name]
    Age: [age]
    Student ID: [student_id]
    Grades: [grades]
    Average Grade: [average_grade]
    ```
    where `[average_grade]` is formatted to two decimal places.

- **Sample Input:**
  - `name = "Alice"`
  - `age = 20`
  - `student_id = "S12345"`
  - `grades = [88, 92, 85, 90, 87]`

- **Sample Output:**
  ```
  Student Name: Alice
  Age: 20
  Student ID: S12345
  Grades: [88, 92, 85, 90, 87]
  Average Grade: 88.40
  ```

```python
class Student:
    def __init__(self, name, age, student_id, grades):
        self._name = name
        self._age = age
        self._student_id = student_id
        self._grades = grades

    def get_name(self):
        """Returns the student's name."""
        return self._name

    def set_name(self, name):
        """Sets the student's name."""
        self._name = name

    def get_age(self):
        """Returns the student's age."""
        return self._age

    def set_age(self, age):
        """Sets the student's age."""
        self._age = age

    def get_student_id(self):
        """Returns the student's ID."""
        return self._student_id

    def set_student_id(self, student_id):
        """Sets the student's ID."""
        self._student_id = student_id

    def get_grades(self):
        """Returns the list of grades."""
        return self._grades

    def set_grades(self, grades):
        """Sets the list of grades."""
        self._grades = grades

    def calculate_average(self):
        """Computes and returns the average of the grades. Returns 0 if the list is empty."""
        if len(self._grades) == 0:
            return 0
        return sum(self._grades) / len(self._grades)

    def display_info(self):
        """Prints the student's information in a readable format."""
        avg_grade = self.calculate_average()
        print(f"Student Name: {self._name}")
        print(f"Age: {self._age}")
        print(f"Student ID: {self._student_id}")
        print(f"Grades: {self._grades}")
        print(f"Average Grade: {avg_grade:.2f}")


# Creating an instance of Student class
student = Student("Alice", 20, "S12345", [88, 92, 85, 90, 87])

# Calling display_info method to print student details
student.display_info()

</think>To find the largest whole number that cannot be expressed as \(6a + 9b + 20c\), where \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) are non-negative integers, we need to determine the largest number that cannot be formed using these coefficients. We will follow a systematic approach to check the expressibility of consecutive numbers and identify the largest number that cannot be expressed.

1. **Identify the smallest coefficient**: The smallest coefficient among 6, 9, and 20 is 6.
