Each class's journey starts by taking a train from the first station. Let's say a path for a class would be a sequence of edges (trains) from station 1 to 2 to 3 ... to n.

So for each step between stations, the departure and arrival times of the train are given. 

For a particular class's path, when moving from station i to i+1, they take a train that departs at x, arrives at y. So, during this journey, they depart at x from station i, so their arrival at station i+1 is at time y.

At the arrival at station j (any j>1), the arrival time is y. The constraint is that no two classes have the same arrival time at the same station j. So, for each station, each time you arrive there, only one class can have that as their arrival time there. 

Therefore, the key is to choose a set of paths (each as a sequence of trains, choosing one train for each step) such that every pair of paths has no shared arrival time at any station. 

At the same time, the maximum number possible, up to g, but choose as many as possible (could it be more? But when given g, it's probably to output the minimum total cost when selecting the maximum possible number, up to g).

So the problem requires:

1. Find the maximum possible k (<=g) which can be chosen such that there exists k paths with all arrival constraints satisfied.

2. Then, compute the minimal total cost of these k paths (each with their own paths).

Hmm, but how do I model this?

An approach might be to model this as a graph where each path represents the journey of a class, and we need to select paths such that no arrival time is duplicated in any of the stations. We need to find the largest such subset of paths, and among those subsets, the minimal cost.

But since the number of paths is potentially large, we can't just enumerate all possible paths. So this seems computationally difficult.

Alternative Idea: This problem is similar to scheduling jobs with constraints. Each possible path (from station 1 to n) is a "job" (representing a class path), and each such job consumes certain "resources"— the arrival times at each station along its path. For two jobs (paths) to not conflict, their resources (arrival times) on each station must not have overlapping times.

Wait, but perhaps each path uses the arrival times at stations along the way as unique "reservations". If two paths share any same arrival time at any station (including different stations?), no—same time at same station would conflict. So any two paths that have even a single overlapping arrival (at the same station, same time) cannot both be chosen. So the paths are required to have, for all pairs of their arrival times, that for all stations j, their arrival time at j is different from the arrival time of the other path at j (but could have different stations with same time if they don't interfere; for instance, path A's arrival at station 2 and path B's arrival at station 3 could be at the same time, but the same station's arrival must be unique.)

Therefore, the constraints are that for any two paths, there does not exist a time t and a station s where both paths arrive at s at time t.

Hence, the problem reduces to choosing a subset of paths (each from station 1 to n) where their arrival times do not conflict, and find as many as possible (with this maximum) the minimal total cost.

So first, I need to find all possible paths from the first station to the nth station, along with their sequence of arrival times at each station.

Then, model this as a problem of selecting a subset of these paths, such that for no two paths and any station s, there is a common arrival time at s. Then want to select as many as possible, up to g, and the minimal total cost.

But the problem is that the number of possible paths might be too large for this approach. Imagine even 100 stations, each with 20 options per step... that would be a lot. Hence this is likely infeasible.

<#sep#>You are given a list of integers `nums` and an integer `target`. Your task is to implement a function `find_target(nums, target)` that returns the index of the first occurrence of `target` in the list `nums`. If the `target` is not present in the list, the function should return `-1`.

**Constraints:**
- The length of the list `nums` will be between 1 and 10^4.
- Each element in `nums` will be an integer between -10^9 and 10^9.
- The `target` will be an integer between -10^9 and 10^9.

**Sample Input 1:**
```
nums = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 2]
target = 2
```
**Sample Output 1:**
```
1
```

**Sample Input 2:**
```
nums = [7, 8, 9, 10]
target = 5
```
**Sample Output 2:**
```
-1
```

```python
def find_target(nums, target):
    """
    Returns the index of the first occurrence of target in the list nums.
    If target is not found, returns -1.
    
    :param nums: List[int] - The list of integers to search within.
    :param target: int - The integer to find in the list.
    :return: int - The index of the first occurrence of target, or -1 if not found.
    """
    try:
        return nums.index(target)
    except ValueError:
        return -1

# Example usage:
nums = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 2]
target = 2
print(find_target(nums, target))  # Output: 1
```<#sep#> 9b + 20c\).

**What is happening:**
BatchGetItem returns all results, regardless of the size of the response.

Specifically:
> A single operation can retrieve up to 16 MB of data, which can contain as many as 100 items. BatchGetItem returns a partial result if the response size limit is exceeded, the table's provisioned throughput is exceeded, or an internal processing failure occurs. If a partial result is returned, the operation returns a value for UnprocessedKeys. You can use this value to retry the operation starting with the next item to get.

Today it seems that although moto does provide an `UnprocessedKeys` key in the response, the value is hard-coded to an empty list.

<#sep#>You are given a string `sentence` consisting of words separated by single spaces. Your task is to write a function `reverse_words(sentence)` that returns a new string with the words in reverse order. Assume that the input string does not contain any leading or trailing spaces and consists solely of words and spaces.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Hello world this is a test"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
"test a is this world Hello"
```

```python
def reverse_words(sentence):
    """
    Reverses the order of words in the given sentence.

    :param sentence: A string containing words separated by single spaces.
    :return: A string with the words in reverse order.
    """
    return ' '.join(sentence.split()[::-1])

# Test the function
sentence = "Hello world this is a test"
print(reverse_words(sentence))  # Output: "test a is this world Hello"
```<#sep#>Evaluate the integral using the hyperbolic substitution \( x = a \sinh(t) \):

\[
\int \frac{x^2}{(x^2 + a^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \, dx
\]

<think>
Okay, so I need to solve the integral of x² over (x² + a²) raised to the 3/2 power, and they want me to use the substitution x = a sinh(t). Alright, let's start by recalling how substitution works with integrals. When substituting, I need to replace x with a sinh(t), and then also replace dx with the derivative of x with respect to t times dt. 

First, let's write down the substitution properly. If x = a sinh(t), then the differential dx would be a cosh(t) dt, because the derivative of sinh(t) is cosh(t), right? So dx = a cosh(t) dt. 

Now, substitute x and dx into the integral. The original integral is ∫ [x² / (x² + a²)^(3/2)] dx. Let's plug in x = a sinh(t). Then, x² becomes (a sinh(t))² = a² sinh²(t). The denominator is (x² + a²)^(3/2). Let's compute x² + a²: that's a² sinh²(t) + a² = a²(sinh²(t) + 1). But I remember that sinh²(t) + 1 is equal to cosh²(t), right? Because of the identity cosh²(t) - sinh²(t) = 1, so sinh²(t) + 1 = cosh²(t). So the denominator becomes (a² cosh²(t))^(3/2). Let's simplify that. 

First, a² raised to the 3/2 power is a^(2*(3/2)) = a^3. Then, cosh²(t) raised to the 3/2 power is cosh^(3)(t). So denominator is a³ cosh³(t). 

So now, the integral becomes numerator (a² sinh²(t)) divided by denominator (a³ cosh³(t)) multiplied by dx (which is a cosh(t) dt). Let me write that out:

Integral = ∫ [a² sinh²(t) / (a³ cosh³(t))] * a cosh(t) dt.

Simplify the coefficients and terms. Let's see: a² divided by a³ is 1/a. Then multiplied by a (from dx) gives (1/a)*a = 1. Then the cosh(t) from dx cancels one cosh(t) in the denominator. So in the denominator, we had cosh³(t), and then we multiply by cosh(t) in the numerator (from dx), so denominator becomes cosh²(t). 

So now the integral simplifies to ∫ [sinh²(t) / cosh²(t)] dt. Because all the a terms canceled out. So that's ∫ tanh²(t) dt, since sinh/cosh is tanh, so squared is tanh squared. 

Hmm, integrating tanh squared t dt. Let's recall the integral of tanh²(t). I remember that there's an identity for hyperbolic functions similar to the trigonometric ones. For trigonometric, tan²θ = sec²θ - 1. Similarly, for hyperbolic, maybe tanh²(t) = 1 - sech²(t). Let me verify that. Yes, because cosh² - sinh² = 1, so dividing both sides by cosh², we get 1 - tanh²(t) = 1/cosh²(t) = sech²(t). Therefore, tanh²(t) = 1 - sech²(t). 

So substituting that into the integral, we have ∫ [1 - sech²(t)] dt. That's much easier to integrate. The integral of 1 dt is t, and the integral of sech²(t) dt is tanh(t), since the derivative of tanh(t) is sech²(t). Therefore, the integral becomes t - tanh(t) + C, where C is the constant of integration.

So now we have to express this back in terms of x. Remember we substituted x = a sinh(t). Let's solve for t in terms of x. From x = a sinh(t), sinh(t) = x/a. Therefore, t = arcsinh(x/a). 

Now, tanh(t) can be expressed in terms of x. Let's recall that tanh(t) = sinh(t)/cosh(t). We have sinh(t) = x/a, and cosh(t) can be found from the identity cosh²(t) - sinh²(t) = 1, so cosh(t) = sqrt(1 + sinh²(t)) = sqrt(1 + (x/a)^2) = sqrt((x² + a²)/a²) = sqrt(x² + a²)/a. So cosh(t) = sqrt(x² + a²)/a.

Therefore, tanh(t) = (x/a) / (sqrt(x² + a²)/a) = x / sqrt(x² + a²).

Putting it all together, the integral is t - tanh(t) + C = arcsinh(x/a) - (x / sqrt(x² + a²)) + C.

But arcsinh can also be written in terms of logarithms. The inverse hyperbolic sine function can be expressed as arcsinh(z) = ln(z + sqrt(z² + 1)). <#sep#>Implement a Python class `Car` with the following specifications:

- **Attributes:**
  - `make`: A string representing the manufacturer of the car.
  - `model`: A string representing the model of the car.
  - `year`: An integer representing the year the car was manufactured.
  - `mileage`: An integer representing the current mileage of the car.

- **Methods:**
  - `update_mileage(new_mileage)`: Updates the mileage of the car. The mileage can only be increased; if `new_mileage` is less than the current mileage, print "Mileage cannot be decreased."
  - `display_info()`: Prints the car's information in the format:
    ```
    Car: <year> <make> <model>
    Mileage: <mileage> miles
    ```

- **Constraints:**
  - The mileage should never be allowed to decrease.

- **Sample Input/Output:**
  ```
  Input:
  car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2020, 15000)
  car.display_info()
  car.update_mileage(16000)
  car.display_info()
  car.update_mileage(14000)

  Output:
  Car: 2020 Toyota Corolla
  Mileage: 15000 miles
  Car: 2020 Toyota Corolla
  Mileage: 16000 miles
  Mileage cannot be decreased.
  ```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, mileage):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the given make, model, year, and mileage.
        
        :param make: str - The manufacturer of the car.
        :param model: str - The model of the car.
        :param year: int - The year the car was manufactured.
        :param mileage: int - The current mileage of the car.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.mileage = mileage

    def update_mileage(self, new_mileage):
        """
        Updates the mileage of the car. The mileage can only be increased.
        
        :param new_mileage: int - The new mileage to be set.
        """
        if new_mileage >= self.mileage:
            self.mileage = new_mileage
        else:
            print("Mileage cannot be decreased.")

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the car's information in the format:
        Car: <year> <make> <model>
        Mileage: <mileage> miles
        """
        print(f"Car: {self.year} {self.make} {self.model}")
        print(f"Mileage: {self.mileage} miles")
```<#sep#>)].

</think>To evaluate the integral \(\int \frac{x^2}{(x^2 + a^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \, dx\) using the substitution \(x = a \sinh(t)\):

1. **Substitute \(x = a \sinh(t)\) and \(dx = a \cosh(t) \, dt\)**:
   \[
   \int \frac{(a \sinh(t))^2}{(a^2 \sinh^2(t) + a^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \cdot a \cosh(t) \, dt
   \]

2. **Simplify the integrand**:
   - Numerator: \(a^2 \sinh^2(t) \cdot a \cosh(t) = a^3 \sinh^2(t) \cosh(t)\)
   - Denominator: \((a^2 (\sinh^2(t) + 1))^{\frac{3}{2}} = (a^2 \cosh^2(t))^{\frac{3}{2}} = a^3 \cosh^3(t)\)
   - The integrand becomes:
     \[
     \int \frac{a^3 \sinh^2(t) \cosh(t)}{a^3 \cosh^3(t)} \, dt = \int \frac{\sinh^2(t)}{\cosh^2(t)} \, dt = \int \tanh^2(t) \, dt
     \]

3. **Use the identity \(\tanh^2(t) = 1 - \text{sech}^2(t)\)**:
   \[
   \int (1 - \text{sech}^2(t)) \, dt = \int 1 \, dt - \int \text{sech}^2(t) \, dt = t - \tanh(t) + C
   \]

4. **Convert back to \(x\)**:
   - From \(x = a \sinh(t)\), we have \(t = \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)\).
   - \(\tanh(t) = \frac{\sinh(t)}{\cosh(t)} = \frac{x/a}{\sqrt{x^2 + a^2}/a} = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + a^2}}\).

5. **Final result**:
   \[
   \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right) - \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + a^2}} + C
   \]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right) - \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + a^2}} + C}

              arn = f"arn:aws:logs:{region_name}:{account_id}:log-group:{log_group_name}"
              client.tag_resource(
                  resourceArn=arn,
                  tags={"my-tag": "my-value"},
              )

          yield client

  def test_client(logs):
      arn = f"arn:aws:logs:{region_name}:{account_id}:log-group:{log_group_name}"
      tags = logs.list_tags_for_resource(resourceArn=arn)["tags"]
      assert tags == {"my-tag": "my-value"}
  ```

  </details>

  Running results in

  ```
  NotImplementedError: The tag_resource action has not been implemented
  ```

  Commenting out the `client.tag_resource(...)` call results in

  ```
  NotImplementedError: The list_tags_for_resource action has not been implemented
  ```

  * https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/reference/services/logs/client/list_tags_for_resource.html
  * https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/reference/services/logs/client/tag_resource.html

     def delete_destination<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a function to classify the sentiment of a given sentence as positive, negative, or neutral. You will utilize a pre-trained sentiment analysis model to achieve this. Your function should be efficient and accurate.

**Input:**
- A single string `sentence` representing the sentence to be analyzed.

**Output:**
- A string representing the sentiment of the sentence, which can be "POSITIVE", "NEGATIVE", or "NEUTRAL".

**Sample Input:**
```
"Despite the harsh weather conditions, the hikers enjoyed their adventurous trip to the mountains."
```

**Sample Output:**
```
POSITIVE
```

```python
from transformers import pipeline

# Load the pre-trained sentiment analysis model with a pipeline
sentiment_pipeline = pipeline("sentiment-analysis", model="siebert/sentiment-roberta-large-3-folds")

def predict_sentiment(sentence):
    """
    Predicts the sentiment of a given sentence using a pre-trained model.

    Parameters:
    sentence (str): The sentence to analyze.

    Returns:
    str: The sentiment of the sentence, which can be "POSITIVE", "NEGATIVE", or "NEUTRAL".
    """
    # Get the sentiment prediction for the given sentence
    result = sentiment_pipeline(sentence)[0]
    # Return the label from the result
    return result['label']

# Test the function with the provided example
sentence = "Despite the harsh weather conditions, the hikers enjoyed their adventurous trip to the mountains."
print(predict_sentiment(sentence))

         # RDS resources
         resource_map: Dict[str<#sep#>You are required to implement a stack data structure using a list. The stack should support the following operations:
- `push(item)`: Adds an item to the top of the stack.
- `pop()`: Removes the item from the top of the stack and returns it. If the stack is empty, raise an `IndexError`.
- `peek()`: Returns the item at the top of the stack without removing it. If the stack is empty, raise an `IndexError`.
- `is_empty()`: Returns `True` if the stack is empty, otherwise `False`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: `push(1), push(2), push(3), peek(), pop(), pop(), is_empty(), pop(), is_empty()`
- Output: `3, 3, 2, False, 1, True`

```python
class Stack:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty stack."""
        self.items = []

    def is_empty(self):
        """Check if the stack is empty."""
        return len(self.items) == 0

    def push(self, item):
        """Add an item to the top of the stack."""
        self.items.append(item)

    def pop(self):
        """Remove and return the item from the top of the stack. Raise IndexError if the stack is empty."""
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("pop from empty stack")
        return self.items.pop()

    def peek(self):
        """Return the item at the top of the stack without removing it. Raise IndexError if the stack is empty."""
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("peek from empty stack")
        return self.items[-1]

# Example usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    stack = Stack()
    stack.push(1)
    stack.push(2)
    stack.push(3)
    print(stack.peek())  # Output: 3
    print(stack.pop())   # Output: 3
    print(stack.pop())   # Output: 2
    print(stack.is_empty())  # Output: False
    print(stack.pop())   # Output: 1
    print(stack.is_empty())  # Output: True

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python generator function named `fibonacci()` that yields an infinite sequence of Fibonacci numbers. The Fibonacci sequence is defined as follows: F(0) = 0, F(1) = 1, and F(n) = F(n-1) + F(n-2) for n > 1. Your function should return a generator object that can be iterated over to produce Fibonacci numbers indefinitely.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- The function does not take any input parameters.
- The output is a generator object that can be used to retrieve Fibonacci numbers one by one.

```python
def fibonacci():
    """
    Generator function to yield Fibonacci numbers indefinitely.
    
    Yields:
        int: The next Fibonacci number in the sequence.
    """
    a, b = 0, 1
    while True:
        yield a
        a, b = b, a + b

The incenter is the intersection of angle bisectors. Coordinates can be found using the formula:

If a triangle has vertices at (x₁,y₁), (x₂,y₂), (x₃,y₃), and sides opposite to these vertices have lengths a, b, c respectively, then the incenter is at:

( (a x₁ + b x₂ + c x₃)/(a + b + c), (a y₁ + b y₂ + c y₃)/(a + b + c) )

So, for triangle YXW:

Y(0,0), X(√1.75,0.5), W(0,1).

If the triangle is labeled as A, B, C, with side lengths a=BC, b=AC, c=AB. Then the incenter is ( (a x_A + b x_B + c x_C ) / (a + b + c ), same for y).

So in triangle YXW, let's label:

A = Y(0,0), B = X(√1.75,0.5), C = W(0,1).

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a recursive function named `calculate_factorial` that computes the factorial of a given non-negative integer `n`. The factorial of a non-negative integer `n` is the product of all positive integers less than or equal to `n`. By definition, the factorial of 0 is 1. Your function should handle this edge case appropriately.

**Input:**
- A single non-negative integer `n` (0 ≤ n ≤ 20).

**Output:**
- Return the factorial of `n`.

**Sample Input:**
```
5
```

**Sample Output:**
```
120
```

```python
def calculate_factorial(n):
    """
    Calculate the factorial of a non-negative integer n using recursion.
    
    Parameters:
    n (int): A non-negative integer whose factorial is to be computed.
    
    Returns:
    int: The factorial of the given integer n.
    """
    if n == 0:
        return 1
    else:
        return n * calculate_factorial(n - 1)

 The edge's capacity is infinite (or a large number) but since the flow is limited by the in/out nodes' capacity, this is okay.

 The edge's cost is the train's cost.

    )
    client.put_item(

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class that manages information about books in a library system. The class should support the following functionalities:

- Initialize a book with its title, author, ISBN, publication year, and price.
- Update the price of the book.
- Calculate the age of the book given the current year.
- Display the book's information in a formatted manner.

**Input:**
- The `Book` class will be instantiated with the following parameters:
  - `title` (string): The title of the book.
  - `author` (string): The author of the book.
  - `ISBN` (string): The ISBN number of the book.
  - `year` (integer): The year the book was published.
  - `price` (float): The price of the book.

**Output:**
- The `display_info` method should print the book's details in the following format:
  ```
  Title: <title>
  Author: <author>
  ISBN: <ISBN>
  Year: <year>
  Price: $<price>
  ```
  where `<price>` is formatted to two decimal places.

**Sample Usage:**
- Create a `Book` object with the title "1984", author "George Orwell", ISBN "9780451524935", year 1949, and price 15.99.
- Display the book's information.
- Update the book's price to 12.99.
- Print the age of the book in the year 2023.
- Display the book's updated information.

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, ISBN, year, price):
        """
        Initialize a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param ISBN: The ISBN number of the book.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        :param price: The price of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.ISBN = ISBN
        self.year = year
        self.price = price

    def update_price(self, new_price):
        """
        Update the price of the book.

        :param new_price: The new price of the book.
        """
        self.price = new_price

    def get_age_of_book(self, current_year):
        """
        Calculate the age of the book given the current year.

        :param current_year: The current year.
        :return: The age of the book.
        """
        return current_year - self.year

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Display the book's information in a formatted manner.
        """
        print(f"Title: {self.title}")
        print(f"Author: {self.author}")
        print(f"ISBN: {self.ISBN}")
        print(f"Year: {self.year}")
        print(f"Price: ${self.price:.2f}")

# Example usage:
# book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949, 15.99)
# book.display_info()
# book.update_price(12.99)
# print("Age of the book in 2023:", book.get_age_of_book(2023))
# book.display_info()

emp_db.update_employee(emp_id1, position="Senior Software Engineer")

print(emp_db.get_all_employees())  # Output: {1: {'name': 'John Doe', 'position': 'Senior Software Engineer', 'salary': 75000}, 2: {'name': 'Jane Smith', 'position': 'Data Scientist', 'salary': 80000}}
emp_db.remove_employee(emp_id2)
print(emp_db.get_all_employees())  # Output: {1: {'name': 'John Doe', 'position': 'Senior Software Engineer', 'salary': 75000}}
```

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self):
        self.employees = {}
        self.employee_id_counter = 1

    def add_employee(self, name, position, salary):
        """
        Adds a new employee with the given name, position, and salary.
        Returns the unique employee ID.
        """
        employee_id = self.employee_id_counter
        self.employees[employee_id] = {"name": name, "position": position, "salary": salary}
        self.employee_id_counter += 1
        return employee_id

    def remove_employee(self, employee_id):
        """
        Removes the employee with the specified ID.
        Returns True if the employee was successfully removed, otherwise False.
        """
        if employee_id in self.employees:
            del self.employees[employee_id]
            return True
        return False

    def update_employee(self, employee_id, name=None, position=None, salary=None):
        """
        Updates the details of the employee with the specified ID.
        Returns True if the employee was successfully updated, otherwise False.
        """
        if employee_id in self.employees:
            if name is not None:
                self.employees[employee_id]["name"] = name
            if position is not None:
                self.employees[employee_id]["position"] = position
            if salary is not None:
                self.employees[employee_id]["salary"] = salary
            return True
        return False

    def get_employee_info(self, employee_id):
        """
        Returns the details of the employee with the specified ID, or None if the employee does not exist.
        """
        return self.employees.get(employee_id, None)

    def get_all_employees(self):
        """
        Returns a dictionary of all employees with their IDs as keys.
        """
        return self.employees

# Sample usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    emp_db = Employee()
    
    # Add employees
    emp_id1 = emp_db.add_employee("John Doe", "Software Engineer", 75000)
    emp_id2 = emp_db.add_employee("Jane Smith", "Data Scientist", 80000)
    
    # Get info about an employee
    print(emp_db.get_employee_info(emp_id1))  # Output: {'name': 'John Doe', 'position': 'Software Engineer', 'salary': 75000}
    
    # Update an employee's position
    emp_db.update_employee(emp_id1, position="Senior Software Engineer")
    print(emp_db.get_employee_info(emp_id1))  # Output: {'name': 'John Doe', 'position': 'Senior Software Engineer', 'salary': 75000}
    
    # Get all employees
    print(emp_db.get_all_employees())  # Output: {1: {'name': 'John Doe', 'position': 'Senior Software Engineer', 'salary': 75000}, 2: {'name': 'Jane Smith', 'position': 'Data Scientist', 'salary': 80000}}
    
    # Remove an employee
    emp_db.remove_employee(emp_id2)
    print(emp_db.get_all_employees())  # Output: {1: {'name': 'John Doe', 'position': 'Senior Software Engineer', 'salary': 75000}}
```<#sep#> )

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `Employee` to manage employee details in a company's database. The class should support the following functionalities:

1. **Initialization**: Create an `Employee` object with attributes `name`, `employee_id`, `department`, and `salary`.
2. **String Representation**: Provide a string representation of the `Employee` object that includes all attributes.
3. **Salary Adjustment**: Implement a method to increase the employee's salary by a specified amount.
4. **Department Transfer**: Implement a method to change the employee's department.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `Employee("John Doe", 12345, "Engineering", 75000)`
- Output: `Employee: John Doe, ID: 12345, Department: Engineering, Salary: 75000`
- After `give_raise(5000)`: `Employee: John Doe, ID: 12345, Department: Engineering, Salary: 80000`
- After `transfer_department("Marketing")`: `Employee: John Doe, ID: 12345, Department: Marketing, Salary: 80000`

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, department, salary):
        """
        Initialize an Employee object with the given name, employee_id, department, and salary.
        
        :param name: str - The name of the employee.
        :param employee_id: int - The unique identifier for the employee.
        :param department: str - The department where the employee works.
        :param salary: float - The salary of the employee.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.department = department
        self.salary = salary

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Return a string representation of the Employee object.
        
        :return: str - A formatted string containing the employee's details.
        """
        return f"Employee: {self.name}, ID: {self.employee_id}, Department: {self.department}, Salary: {self.salary}"

    def give_raise(self, amount):
        """
        Increase the employee's salary by the specified amount.
        
        :param amount: float - The amount by which to increase the salary.
        """
        self.salary += amount

    def transfer_department(self, new_department):
        """
        Change the employee's department to the new specified department.
        
        :param new_department: str - The new department for the employee.
        """
        self.department = new_department

The line AB is YX: from Y(0,0) to X(√1.75,0.5). Parametric equations for AB: any point can be written as (√1.75 * s, 0.5s ) for s ∈ [0,1].

The foot F is the projection of I onto AB. The formula for the parameter s is given by the dot product:

s = (I ⋅ (X - Y)) / |X - Y|².

Where I is vector (x_I, y_I ), X - Y is vector (√1.75,0.5 ).

Compute I ⋅ (X - Y )=x_I *√1.75 + y_I *0.5.

Sum≈0.456 +0.25=0.706,

Divide by 2:0.353.

Therefore, s≈0.353.

Thus, F=(√1.75 *0.353,0.5 *0.353 )≈(1.322*0.353,0.1765 )≈(0.467,0.1765).

Therefore, F≈(0.467,0.1765).

<#sep#>You are given a string `text` and a list of `stop_words`. Your task is to implement a function `filter_words` that processes the string by removing all occurrences of the stop words, ignoring case and punctuation. The function should return the modified string.

**Input:**
- A string `text` (1 ≤ |text| ≤ 1000) containing words separated by spaces and possibly including punctuation.
- A list of `stop_words` (0 ≤ |stop_words| ≤ 100), where each stop word is a string.

**Output:**
- A string with all stop words removed, maintaining the original order of the remaining words.

**Sample Input:**
```
text = "Hello, world! How's it going?"
stop_words = ["hello", "world"]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
"How's it going?"
```

```python
import string

def filter_words(text, stop_words):
    """
    Removes all occurrences of stop words from the input text, ignoring case and punctuation.
    
    :param text: A string containing words separated by spaces and possibly including punctuation.
    :param stop_words: A list of strings representing the stop words to be removed.
    :return: A string with all stop words removed.
    """
    stop_words_set = set(word.lower() for word in stop_words)
    translator = str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation)
    cleaned_text = text.translate(translator)
    filtered_words = [word for word in cleaned_text.split() if word.lower() not in stop_words_set]
    return ' '.join(filtered_words)

# Example usage
text = "Hello, world! How's it going?"
stop_words = ["hello", "world"]
print(filter_words(text, stop_words))  # Output: "How's it going?"
```<#sep#>.6467 ).

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should manage the details of a book including its title, author, ISBN, total number of copies, and the number of copies currently available. The class should support borrowing and returning books, updating the number of available copies accordingly.

**Methods to Implement:**
- `__init__(self, title, author, isbn, total_copies)`: Initializes a new book with the given title, author, ISBN, and total number of copies. Initially, all copies are available.
- `__str__(self)`: Returns a string representation of the book in the format: `"Title: {title}, Author: {author}, ISBN: {isbn}, Copies Available: {copies_available}, Total Copies: {total_copies}"`.
- `borrow(self)`: Decreases the number of available copies by one if there are any available. If no copies are available, it should return the string `"Error: No copies available to borrow."`.
- `return_book(self)`: Increases the number of available copies by one if it is less than the total number of copies.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 3)
print(book)  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935, Copies Available: 3, Total Copies: 3
book.borrow()
print(book)  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935, Copies Available: 2, Total Copies: 3
book.return_book()
print(book)  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935, Copies Available: 3, Total Copies: 3
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, total_copies):
        """
        Initializes a new book with the given title, author, ISBN, and total number of copies.
        Initially, all copies are available.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.total_copies = total_copies
        self.copies_available = total_copies

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the book.
        """
        return (f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, "
                f"ISBN: {self.isbn}, Copies Available: {self.copies_available}, "
                f"Total Copies: {self.total_copies}")

    def borrow(self):
        """
        Decreases the number of available copies by one if there are any available.
        If no copies are available, it returns the string "Error: No copies available to borrow."
        """
        if self.copies_available > 0:
            self.copies_available -= 1
        else:
            return "Error: No copies available to borrow."

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Increases the number of available copies by one if it is less than the total number of copies.
        """
        if self.copies_available < self.total_copies:
            self.copies_available += 1
```<#sep#>In the complex plane, let points \( A(x) \), \( B(y) \), and \( C(z) \) be such that \( |x| = |y| = |z| = m \) where \( m \in \mathbb{R}^+ \), and \( m^2 (x + y + z) = xyz \in \mathbb{R} \). Find the area of triangle \( ABC \).

First, since |x| = |y| = |z| = m, we can write x = m * e^(iθ₁), y = m * e^(iθ₂), z = m * e^(iθ₃), right? Because any complex number with modulus m can be expressed in exponential form with magnitude m and some angle θ. 

Given that m²(x + y + z) = xyz, let's substitute the exponential forms into this equation. Let me write that out:

m²(m e^(iθ₁) + m e^(iθ₂) + m e^(iθ₃)) = (m e^(iθ₁))(m e^(iθ₂))(m e^(iθ₃))

Simplify both sides. Left side: m² * m (e^(iθ₁) + e^(iθ₂) + e^(iθ₃)) = m³ (e^(iθ₁) + e^(iθ₂) + e^(iθ₃))

Right side: m * m * m * e^(i(θ₁ + θ₂ + θ₃)) = m³ e^(i(θ₁ + θ₂ + θ₃))

So, set both sides equal:

e^(iθ₁) + e^(iθ₂) + e^(iθ₃) = e^(i(θ₁ + θ₂ + θ₃))

<#sep#>You are given a dictionary where the keys are student names and the values are their corresponding grades. Your task is to write a Python function named `sort_students_by_grade` that sorts this dictionary by grades in descending order and returns a list of tuples containing the student names and their grades. Ensure that all grades are numeric values. If any grade is not numeric, the function should handle the error gracefully and return an empty list.

**Input:**
- A dictionary `students` where keys are strings (student names) and values are numeric grades (integers or floats).

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains a student name and their grade, sorted by grade in descending order.

**Sample Input:**
```python
{"Alice": 90, "Bob": 85, "Charlie": 95, "David": 80}
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[('Charlie', 95), ('Alice', 90), ('Bob', 85), ('David', 80)]
```

```python
def sort_students_by_grade(students):
    """
    Sorts a dictionary of student names and their corresponding grades
    in descending order by grade and returns a list of tuples.

    Args:
        students (dict): A dictionary with student names as keys and numeric grades as values.

    Returns:
        list: A list of tuples sorted by grade in descending order.
    """
    try:
        # Ensure all grades are numeric
        for grade in students.values():
            if not isinstance(grade, (int, float)):
                raise ValueError("All grades must be numeric values.")

        # Sort the dictionary by grade in descending order and convert to a list of tuples
        return sorted(students.items(), key=lambda item: item[1], reverse=True)

    except ValueError:
        return []

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Library` class in Python that manages a collection of books. The class should support the following operations:

- `add_book(title)`: Adds a new book with the given title to the library if it does not already exist. If the book is added successfully, print "Book '{title}' added successfully." Otherwise, print "Book '{title}' already exists in the library."
- `remove_book(title)`: Removes a book with the given title from the library if it exists. If the book is removed successfully, print "Book '{title}' removed successfully." Otherwise, print "Book '{title}' not found in the library."
- `list_books()`: Lists all books in the library along with their statuses. If there are no books, print "No books in the library."
- `checkout_book(title)`: Checks out a book with the given title if it is available. If the book is checked out successfully, print "Book '{title}' checked out successfully." If the book is already checked out, print "Book '{title}' is already checked out." If the book does not exist, print "Book '{title}' not found in the library."
- `return_book(title)`: Returns a book with the given title if it was checked out. If the book is returned successfully, print "Book '{title}' returned successfully." If the book was not checked out, print "Book '{title}' was not checked out." If the book does not exist, print "Book '{title}' not found in the library."

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
lib = Library()
lib.add_book("1984")
lib.list_books()
lib.checkout_book("1984")
lib.list_books()
lib.return_book("1984")
lib.list_books()
lib.remove_book("1984")
lib.list_books()
```

**Expected Output:**
```
Book '1984' added successfully.
Book: '1984' - Status: Available
Book '1984' checked out successfully.
Book: '1984' - Status: Checked Out
Book '1984' returned successfully.
Book: '1984' - Status: Available
Book '1984' removed successfully.
No books in the library.
```

```python
class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize a dictionary to store books and their status."""
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, title):
        """Add a book with an 'Available' status if it does not already exist."""
        if title not in self.books:
            self.books[title] = "Available"
            print(f"Book '{title}' added successfully.")
        else:
            print(f"Book '{title}' already exists in the library.")

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """Remove a book from the library if it exists."""
        if title in self.books:
            del self.books[title]
            print(f"Book '{title}' removed successfully.")
        else:
            print(f"Book '{title}' not found in the library.")

    def list_books(self):
        """List all books and their statuses."""
        if not self.books:
            print("No books in the library.")
        else:
            for title, status in self.books.items():
                print(f"Book: '{title}' - Status: {status}")

    def checkout_book(self, title):
        """Check out a book if it is available."""
        if title in self.books:
            if self.books[title] == "Available":
                self.books[title] = "Checked Out"
                print(f"Book '{title}' checked out successfully.")
            else:
                print(f"Book '{title}' is already checked out.")
        else:
            print(f"Book '{title}' not found in the library.")

    def return_book(self, title):
        """Return a book if it was checked out."""
        if title in self.books:
            if self.books[title] == "Checked Out":
                self.books[title] = "Available"
                print(f"Book '{title}' returned successfully.")
            else:
                print(f"Book '{title}' was not checked out.")
        else:
            print(f"Book '{title}' not found in the library.")
```<#sep#> cos(0°) = 1. Then the sum is (-1) + (-1) + 1 = -1. That works.

 这是<#sep#>Moto doesn't base64 decode messages before signing them
Hello, we've been testing some of our KMS interactions with Moto and have run across a weird inconsistency. I've found that Moto is not base64 decoding the messages before signing them--AWS does. Its hard to notice it when you're using moto for both the sign and verify operations because it reflected on both sides and it cancels itself out. In our case, we're working with JWTs and trying to verify their public keys independently of Moto/KMS. Is this as simple as dropping a one line encode/decode in the related sign/verify in the KMS backed? Here's a reproduction:
```python
import base64
import cryptography
from moto import mock_kms
from moto.kms.models import KmsBackend
import boto3
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives import serialization, hashes
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives.asymmetric import padding
from cryptography.hazmat.primitives.asymmetric.ec import ECDSA
from unittest.mock import patch


def unbase64_the_input_because_kms_actually_does_this(original_function):
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        kwargs['message'] = base64.b64decode(kwargs['message'])
        return original_function(*args, **kwargs)

    return wrapper


@mock_kms
def problem(signing_algorithm: str = 'ECDSA_SHA_256', show_moto_workaround: bool = True):
    kms_client = boto3.client('kms', region_name='us-east-1')
    response = kms_client.create_key(
        Description="example",
        KeyUsage='SIGN_VERIFY',
        CustomerMasterKeySpec='ECC_NIST_P256' if 'ECDSA' in signing_algorithm else 'RSA_2048',
    )
    key_id = response['KeyMetadata']['KeyId']
    public_key = kms_client.get_public_key(KeyId=key_id)['PublicKey']

    message = b'example message'
    response = kms_client.sign(
        KeyId=key_id,
        Message=message,
        MessageType='RAW',
        SigningAlgorithm=signing_algorithm,
    )

    if signing_algorithm == 'ECDSA_SHA_256':
        raw_signature = response['Signature']
        sign_kwargs = dict(signature_algorithm=ECDSA(hashes.SHA256()))
    elif signing_algorithm == 'RSASSA_PSS_SHA_256':
        raw_signature = response['Signature']
        sign_kwargs = dict(
            padding=padding.PSS(mgf=padding.MGF1(hashes.SHA256()), salt_length=padding.PSS.MAX_LENGTH),
            algorithm=hashes.SHA256(),
        )
    else:
        raise RuntimeError("Unsupported for toy problem")

    # KMS doesn't do this, but moto does.
    if show_moto_workaround:
        serialization.load_der_public_key(public_key).verify(
            signature=raw_signature, data=base64.urlsafe_b64encode(message), **sign_kwargs
        )

    # Moto does this instead and it causes invalid signatures
    try:
        serialization.load_der_public_key(public_key).verify(signature=raw_signature, data=message, **sign_kwargs)
        print(f"{signing_algorithm}: Moto KMS must have been patched, because this should have failed")
    except cryptography.exceptions.InvalidSignature:
        print(f"{signing_algorithm}: Failed to verify signature of non-base64 encoded message")


if __name__ == '__main__':
    problem(signing_algorithm='ECDSA_SHA_256')
    problem(signing_algorithm='RSASSA_PSS_SHA_256')

    # The way we've been testing
    with patch(
        'moto.kms.models.KmsBackend.sign',
        unbase64_the_input_because_kms_actually_does_this(KmsBackend.sign),
    ):
        problem(signing_algorithm='ECDSA_SHA_256', show_moto_workaround=False)
```

<#sep#>You are given a Python dictionary representing a nested JSON object. Your task is to implement a function named `flatten_json` that recursively flattens the JSON object into a single-level dictionary. The keys in the resulting dictionary should be the concatenation of the original keys, separated by a dot (.) if they are nested. For example, given the input `{"a": {"b": 1, "c": {"d": 2}}}`, the output should be `{"a.b": 1, "a.c.d": 2}`.

**Sample Input:**
```python
{"name": "John", "address": {"city": "New York", "zip": 10001}, "contact": {"email": "john@example.com", "phone": "123-456-7890"}}
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{'name': 'John', 'address.city': 'New York', 'address.zip': 10001, 'contact.email': 'john@example.com', 'contact.phone': '123-456-7890'}
```

```python
def flatten_json(y):
    """
    Recursively flattens a nested JSON object into a single-level dictionary.
    
    Parameters:
    y (dict): The nested JSON object to be flattened.
    
    Returns:
    dict: A single-level dictionary with concatenated keys.
    """
    out = {}

    def flatten(x, name=''):
        if isinstance(x, dict):
            for a in x:
                flatten(x[a], name + a + '.')
        elif isinstance(x, list):
            for i, a in enumerate(x):
                flatten(a, name + str(i) + '.')
        else:
            out[name[:-1]] = x

    flatten(y)
    return out

# Example usage:
nested_json = {"name": "John", "address": {"city": "New York", "zip": 10001}, "contact": {"email": "john@example.com", "phone": "123-456-7890"}}
flattened_json = flatten_json(nested_json)
print(flattened_json)

<#sep#>You are given a list of sentences. Your task is to implement a function `find_common_words` that identifies the set of common words across all sentences, ignoring case sensitivity and common English stop words. The function should return a list of these common words sorted alphabetically.

**Input:**
- A list of strings, where each string is a sentence.

**Output:**
- A list of strings, representing the common words sorted alphabetically.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.",
    "A quick brown dog outpaces a quick fox.",
    "Over the lazy dog, the quick fox jumps."
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
['brown', 'fox', 'quick']
```

```python
import re
from string import punctuation

def find_common_words(sentences):
    """
    Finds the set of common words across all sentences, ignoring case sensitivity and common English stop words.
    
    :param sentences: List of strings, where each string is a sentence.
    :return: List of strings, representing the common words sorted alphabetically.
    """
    # Define a set of common English stop words
    stop_words = set([
        "i", "me", "my", "myself", "we", "our", "ours", "ourselves", "you", "your", "yours", "yourself", 
        "yourselves", "he", "him", "his", "himself", "she", "her", "hers", "herself", "it", "its", "itself", 
        "they", "them", "their", "theirs", "themselves", "what", "which", "who", "whom", "this", "that", 
        "these", "those", "am", "is", "are", "was", "were", "be", "been", "being", "have", "has", "had", 
        "having", "do", "does", "did", "doing", "a", "an", "the", "and", "but", "if", "or", "because", "as", 
        "until", "while", "of", "at", "by", "for", "with", "about", "against", "between", "into", "through", 
        "during", "before", "after", "above", "below", "to", "from", "up", "down", "in", "out", "on", "off", 
        "over", "under", "again", "further", "then", "once", "here", "there", "when", "where", "why", "how", 
        "all", "any", "both", "each", "few", "more", "most", "other", "some", "such", "no", "nor", "not", 
        "only", "own", "same", "so", "than", "too", "very", "s", "t", "can", "will", "just", "don", "should", 
        "now"
    ])
    
    # Initialize a list to hold the word sets for each sentence
    word_sets = []
    
    # Process each sentence
    for sentence in sentences:
        # Convert to lowercase
        sentence = sentence.lower()
        # Remove punctuation using regex
        sentence = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', sentence)
        # Split the sentence into words
        words = sentence.split()
        # Filter out stop words and create a set of words
        filtered_words = set(word for word in words if word not in stop_words)
        # Add the set to the list
        word_sets.append(filtered_words)
    
    # Find the intersection of all sets to get common words
    common_words = set.intersection(*word_sets)
    
    # Return the sorted list of common words
    return sorted(common_words)
```<#sep#> the area is m², and degenerate cases (area zero) might be excluded if the problem implies a triangle. So perhaps the answer is m².

Alternatively, let's consider the area formula in terms of complex numbers. Let's recall that the area of triangle ABC with vertices x, y, z is given by (1/4i) | (x - y)(\overline{y} - \overline{z}) + (y - z)(\overline{z} - \overline{x}) + (z - x)(\overline{x} - \overline{y}) |. Or, alternatively, there's a formula using the imaginary part of the product of differences.

But maybe using vectors. Since all points lie on a circle of radius m, perhaps the area can be expressed in terms of the sine of angles between them.

But given the condition that a + b + c = abc where |a|=|b|=|c|=1, and given that in the example this leads to area m², which seems like the maximal area for a triangle inscribed in a circle of radius m (which is (3√3/4)m² for an equilateral triangle). But wait, in our example, the area is m², which is larger than (3√3/4)m² ≈1.299m². Wait, no, wait, for example, m=1, area is 1, which is less than 1.299. Wait, confused.

Wait, maximum area of a triangle inscribed in a circle of radius m is achieved by the equilateral triangle, with area (3√3/4)m² ≈1.299m². In our case, the example triangle has area 1*m², which is less than that. So not maximum.

But why the area is m². Let's confirm again. If vertices are at (m,0), (0,m), (0,-m), then it's a right-angled triangle with legs of length m√2 each, but no. Wait, the distances:

Between (m,0) and (0,m): √[(m)^2 + (m)^2] = m√2.

Between (0,m) and (0,-m): 2m.

Between (m,0) and (0,-m): same as above, m√2.

But the area is computed as 1/2 * base * height. If we take the base as 2m (from (0,m) to (0,-m)), the height is the distance from (m,0) to the line x=0, which is m. So area is 1/2 * 2m * m = m². Correct.

So yes, that triangle has area m². So maybe that's the only solution? If the problem's conditions enforce that the triangle has to be right-angled with legs of m and 2m? Or perhaps more generally, the area is m². 

But let's think again about the original condition. If the equation a + b + c = abc holds, with |a|=|b|=|c|=1, then when we considered the case with one real and two conjugate imaginary, the area was m². If there are other solutions, perhaps with different configurations, do they yield the same area?

Alternatively, perhaps regardless of the specific positions, the area is m². Let's check another example. Suppose we take x = m, y = mω, z = mω², where ω is a cube root of unity. Then x + y + z = m(1 + ω + ω²) = 0. xyz = m³*1*ω*ω² = m³. So m²(x + y + z) = 0 = xyz? Only possible if m=0, which is not in R⁺. So this configuration doesn't work.

Alternatively, take three points such that two are reflections of each other over the real axis. Let x = m, y = me^(iθ), z = me^(-iθ). Then x + y + z = m + 2m cosθ. xyz = m³. So equation m²(m + 2m cosθ) = m³. Simplify: m³(1 + 2 cosθ) = m³ ⇒ 1 + 2 cosθ =1 ⇒ cosθ=0 ⇒ θ=π/2 or 3π/2. Then y = mi, z = -mi. Which brings us back to the previous case. So area is m².

Thus, this suggests that the only non-degenerate solution is the case where one point is on the real axis and the other two are conjugate imaginaries, leading to area m². Therefore, the area of triangle ABC is m².

**Final Answer**
\boxed{m^2}
</think>Given points \( A(x) \), \( B(y) \), and \( C(z) \) in the complex plane with \( |x| = |y| = |z| = m \) where \( m \in \mathbb{R}^+ \), and the condition \( m^2 (x + y + z) = xyz \in \mathbb{R} \), we need to find the area of triangle \( ABC \).

1. **Complex Number Representation**:
   Since \( |x| = |y| = |z| = m \), we can write:
   \[
   x = m e^{i\theta_1}, \quad y = m e^{i\theta_2}, \quad z = m e^{i\theta_3}
   \]
   Substituting these into the given equation \( m^2 (x + y + z) = xyz \), we get:
   \[
   m^3 (e^{i\theta_1} + e^{i\theta_2} + e^{i\theta_3}) = m^3 e^{i(\theta_1 + \theta_2 + \theta_3)}
   \]
   Simplifying, we find:
   \[
   e^{i\theta_1} + e^{i\theta_2} + e^{i\theta_3} = e^{i(\theta_1 + \theta_2 + \theta_3)}
   \]

2. **Analyzing the Equation**:
   This equation implies that the sum of three unit vectors equals another unit vector. A specific solution is when one of the points is real and the other two are complex conjugates. For example, let \( x = m \), \( y = mi \), and \( z = -mi \).

3. **Verification**:
   For \( x = m \), \( y = mi \), and \( z = -mi \):
   - The left-hand side \( m^2 (x + y + z) = m^2 (m + mi - mi) = m^3 \).
   - The right-hand side \( xyz = m \cdot mi \cdot (-mi) = m^3 \).
   - This satisfies the given condition.

4. **Area Calculation**:
   The vertices of the triangle are \( (m, 0) \), \( (0, m) \), and \( (0, -m) \).
   - The base of the triangle is the vertical distance between \( (0, m) \) and \( (0, -m) \), which is \( 2m \).
   - The height of the triangle is the horizontal distance from \( (m, 0) \) to the y-axis, which is \( m \).
   - The area of the triangle is:
     \[
     \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times \text{base} \times \text{height} = \frac{1}{2} \times 2m \times m = m^2
     \]

Thus, the area of triangle \( ABC \) is \(\boxed{m^2}\).<#sep#>('\n')[n-1]; }

To solve this problem, we need to create a program or function that can return its own code's nth line. This requires the program to be self-referential without accessing its source file directly. The solution involves using the `toString()` method to retrieve the function's source code, splitting it into lines, and returning the requested line.

### Approach
The approach involves creating a function that uses its own `toString()` method to get its source code as a string. By splitting this string into lines using `split('\n')`, the function can return the nth line (adjusting for 1-based indexing). This approach ensures that the function is self-contained and does not rely on external files, adhering to the problem constraints.

### Solution Code
```javascript
function f(n) { return (f.toString().split('\n'))[n-1] }
```

### Explanation
1. **Function Definition**: The function `f` is defined to take an input `n`.
2. **ToString Method**: `f.toString()` retrieves the source code of the function as a string.
3. **Splitting Lines**: The string is split into an array of lines using `split('\n')`.
4. **Return Requested Line**: The nth line (adjusted for 1-based indexing) is returned from the array.

This solution ensures that the function meets all the problem requirements, including being at least 4 lines long, each line unique and non-empty, and not relying on accessing the file system. The use of `toString()` allows the function to self-reference its own code, making it both self-aware and minimal in code length.<#sep#>Cognito REFRESH_TOKEN auth raises incorrect error for invalid token

# Full Code
```
from unittest import TestCase

import boto3
import botocore
from moto import mock_cognitoidp


@mock_cognitoidp
class TestCognitoUserDeleter(TestCase):
    def setUp(self) -> None:
        self.cognito_client = boto3.client('cognito-idp')

        self.user_pool_id = self.cognito_client.create_user_pool(PoolName='test-pool')['UserPool']['Id']

        self.user_pool_client_id = self.cognito_client.create_user_pool_client(
            UserPoolId=self.user_pool_id,
            ClientName='test-client'
        )['UserPoolClient']['ClientId']

    def test_should(self):
        self.cognito_client.admin_create_user(
            UserPoolId=self.user_pool_id,
            Username='foo',
            TemporaryPassword='bar'
        )

        self.cognito_client.admin_set_user_password(
            UserPoolId=self.user_pool_id,
            Username='foo',
            Password='bar',
            Permanent=True
        )

        response = self.cognito_client.admin_initiate_auth(
            UserPoolId=self.user_pool_id,
            ClientId=self.user_pool_client_id,
            AuthFlow='ADMIN_USER_PASSWORD_AUTH',
            AuthParameters={
                'USERNAME': 'foo',
                'PASSWORD': 'bar'
            }
        )

        refresh_token = response['AuthenticationResult']['RefreshToken']
        print(refresh_token)

        self.cognito_client.admin_user_global_sign_out(
            UserPoolId=self.user_pool_id,
            Username='foo'
        )

        with self.assertRaises(botocore.exceptions.ClientError) as context:
            self.cognito_client.admin_initiate_auth(
                UserPoolId=self.user_pool_id,
                ClientId=self.user_pool_client_id,
                AuthFlow='REFRESH_TOKEN',
                AuthParameters={
                    'REFRESH_TOKEN': refresh_token,
                }
            )

        self.assertEqual(
            'NotAuthorizedException',
            context.exception.response['Error']['Code']
        )
```

Your goal is to add a new method `stop_engine()` to this class that outputs "Engine stopped". Your implementation should be such that when `stop_engine()` is called on an instance of `Car`, it prints "Engine stopped".

my_car.start_engine()
my_car.stop_engine()

Engine stopped

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the specified make, model, and year.

        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year

    def start_engine(self):
        """
        Simulates starting the car's engine by printing "Engine started".
        """
        print("Engine started")

    def stop_engine(self):
        """
        Simulates stopping the car's engine by printing "Engine stopped".
        """
        print("Engine stopped")

The i-th point has coordinates (x_i, y_i) such that x_i > 0 and y_i > 0. The coordinates are not necessarily integer.

In one move you perform the following operations: 

  * choose two points a and b (a ≠ b); 
  * move point a from (x_a, y_a) to either (x_a + 1, y_a) or (x_a, y_a + 1); 
  * move point b from (x_b, y_b) to either (x_b + 1, y_b) or (x_b, y_b + 1); 
  * remove points a and b. 



However, the move can only be performed if there exists a line that passes through the new coordinates of a, new coordinates of b and (0, 0).

Otherwise, the move can't be performed and the points stay at their original coordinates (x_a, y_a) and (x_b, y_b), respectively.

The numeration of points does not change after some points are removed. Once the points are removed, they can't be chosen in any later moves. Note that you have to move both points during the move, you can't leave them at their original coordinates.

What is the maximum number of moves you can perform? What are these moves?

If there are multiple answers, you can print any of them.
Input

The first line contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of points.

The i-th of the next n lines contains four integers a_i, b_i, c_i, d_i (1 ≤ a_i, b_i, c_i, d_i ≤ 10^9). The coordinates of the i-th point are x_i = (a_i)/(b_i) and y_i = (c_i)/(d_i).

Output

In the first line print a single integer c — the maximum number of moves you can perform.

Each of the next c lines should contain a description of a move: two integers a and b (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) — the points that are removed during the current move. There should be a way to move points a and b according to the statement so that there's a line that passes through the new coordinates of a, the new coordinates of b and (0, 0). No removed point can be chosen in a later move.

If there are multiple answers, you can print any of them. You can print the moves and the points in the move in the arbitrary order.

Examples

Output

2 1 1 2
1 2 1 2


Output


1
1 2


Input

69 21 144 63
148 12 105 6


Output


1
2 4

gpt: <think> Okay, let's tackle this problem. So, the task is to find the maximum number of moves we can perform by choosing two points each time and moving them such that their new positions lie on a line passing through the origin. Hmm. 

First, let's understand the move condition. Each move requires selecting two points a and b. After moving each to either right or up, their new coordinates (a's new x or y, and similarly for b) must lie on a line that passes through (0,0). So, the line equation would be y = kx for some slope k, right? So for the new coordinates of a and b, after moving a to (xa+Δx_a, ya + Δy_a) where Δx_a is 1 and Δy_a is 0 or vice versa (same for Δ for b?), actually wait the move must choose either (x_a+1,y_a) or (x_a,y_a+1) for each point. 

Wait, each point must choose their direction. So the new positions are (x_a + 1, y_a) or (x_a, y_a + 1) for point a, and similarly for b. Then, these new coordinates of a and b must lie on the same line through (0,0). 

So, the key here is that for the pair of new points (a_new and b_new) to be colinear with the origin. For this to happen, the line that connects (0,0) must pass through both new positions. 

So, let me formalize this condition: Let’s denote the new coordinates of a as (x_a + delta_x_a, y_a + delta_y_a), where (delta_x_a, delta_y_a) is either (1, 0) or (0,1). Similarly for b. 

The two new points must lie on the same line through (0,0). So the line can be represented as y = kx. So for point a's new coordinates (x_a', y_a') and b's (x_b', y_b'), it must hold that (y_a') / x_a' = y_b' / x_b' = k. 

Alternatively, rearranged, (x_a') * y_b' = (x_b') * y_a'. 

Hmm. So the cross product must be zero. 

So, to satisfy the condition, for the two new points (a's new and b's new), their coordinates must be such that (x_a_new * y_b_new - x_b_new * y_a_new) = 0. 

The move can be made only if there exist some choices of directions (either +1 in x or in y for each) so that this condition holds for those two new points.

Therefore for each pair of points, we need to check whether we can choose directions such that after moving each in one direction, the point's new coordinates satisfy the ratio condition.

Now, the problem is to select as many disjoint pairs of points as possible such that each pair can be "moved appropriately" to satisfy the ratio condition. The goal is to maximize the number of such pairs.

Wait, but how do we model this to get an efficient solution, given n up to 2e5? 

Hmm. 

First, perhaps we can represent each point in terms of their possible targets. Let's think: when moving a point, how can we choose between its two possible new coordinates?

Each point can have two possible new coordinates after being moved. Let me denote:

For a point (x,y), the two possible new positions are:

1. (x + 1, y) → which we can call option1: (x+1, y)
2. (x, y + 1) → option2: (x, y+1)

The key is, for a pair of points a and b, we need to choose an option for each (either 1 or 2 for a and similarly for b) such that the cross product between the two new points is zero. 

So for each pair a and b, if there exists such a combination of their moves (so one can choose between option1/2 for each), then this pair can be considered as a candidate for a move. The problem reduces to choosing maximum edges in a graph where each edge represents a pair that can form a valid move. But this is not exactly right, since each pair can contribute at most once (as once chosen, the points are removed).

But the problem is to find the maximum matching here, but the edges are only available if such a combination exists, and the structure might allow an efficient way to compute the max matching.

```
    def get_caller_identity(self, access_key_id: str) -> Tuple[str, str, str]:
        assumed_role = self.get_assumed_role_from_access_key(access_key_id)
        if assumed_role:
            return assumed_role.user_id, assumed_role.arn, assumed_role.account_id

        iam_backend = iam_backends[self.account_id]["global"]
        user = iam_backend.get_user_from_access_key_id(access_key_id)
        if user:
            return user.id, user.arn, user.account_id

        # Default values in case the request does not use valid credentials generated by moto
        user_id = "AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE"
        arn = f"arn:aws:sts::{self.account_id}:user/moto"
        return user_id, arn, self.account_id

    partition = boto3.Session().get_partition_for_region(region_name=region)

**Input:**
- An integer `n` (0 ≤ n ≤ 50).

**Output:**
- The nth Fibonacci number.

**Sample Input:**
```
10
```

**Sample Output:**
```
55
```

```python
def calculate_fibonacci(n, memo={}):
    """
    Computes the nth Fibonacci number using memoization.

    Parameters:
    n (int): The position in the Fibonacci sequence.

    Returns:
    int: The nth Fibonacci number.
    """
    if n in memo:
        return memo[n]
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    elif n == 1:
        return 1
    else:
        memo[n] = calculate_fibonacci(n-1, memo) + calculate_fibonacci(n-2, memo)
        return memo[n]

<#sep#>You are given a string `s`. Your task is to implement a function `word_count` that processes the string and returns a dictionary containing the frequency of each word. Words are defined as sequences of alphanumeric characters, and the function should be case-insensitive, ignoring punctuation. 

**Input:**
- A single string `s` (1 ≤ |s| ≤ 10^5) containing alphanumeric characters, spaces, and punctuation.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where keys are words in lowercase and values are their respective frequencies in the input string.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Hello, world! Hello, Universe."
```

**Sample Output:**
```
{'hello': 2, 'world': 1, 'universe': 1}
```

```python
import re
from collections import defaultdict

def word_count(input_string):
    """
    Processes a string to count the frequency of each word, ignoring case and punctuation.
    
    Parameters:
    input_string (str): The string to be processed.
    
    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary with words as keys and their frequencies as values.
    """
    # Normalize the input string: convert to lower case and replace non-alphanumeric characters
    normalized_string = re.sub(r'[^a-z0-9\s]', '', input_string.lower())
    # Split the string into words
    words = normalized_string.split()
    # Initialize a default dictionary to store word counts
    count_dict = defaultdict(int)
    # Count each word
    for word in words:
        count_dict[word] += 1
    return dict(count_dict)

# Example usage:
input_string = "Hello, world! Hello, Universe."
print(word_count(input_string))  # Output: {'hello': 2, 'world': 1, 'universe': 1}
```<#sep#>| = product (1 - 1/(3k -1)). </think>To analyze the convergence of the sequence \( a_n = \frac{1 \cdot 4 \cdot \ldots \cdot (3n-2)}{2 \cdot 5 \cdot \ldots \cdot (3n-1)} \), we can proceed as follows:

1. **Logarithmic Transformation**:
   Consider the natural logarithm of \( a_n \):
   \[
   \ln(a_n) = \sum_{k=1}^n \ln\left(\frac{3k-2}{3k-1}\right) = \sum_{k=1}^n \ln\left(1 - \frac{1}{3k-1}\right)
   \]
   For small \( x \), \( \ln(1 - x) \approx -x \). Therefore,
   \[
   \ln\left(1 - \frac{1}{3k-1}\right) \approx -\frac{1}{3k-1}
   \]
   Hence,
   \[
   \ln(a_n) \approx -\sum_{k=1}^n \frac{1}{3k-1}
   \]
   The series \( \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{1}{3k-1} \) diverges because it behaves like the harmonic series \( \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{1}{k} \), which diverges. Therefore, \( \ln(a_n) \to -\infty \) as \( n \to \infty \), implying that \( a_n \to 0 \).

2. **Comparison with Divergent Series**:
   The product terms \( \left(1 - \frac{1}{3k-1}\right) \) are each less than 1. The series \( \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{1}{3k-1} \) diverges, which implies that the infinite product \( \prod_{k=1}^\infty \left(1 - \frac{1}{3k-1}\right) \) converges to 0.

3. **Gamma Function Asymptotics**:
   Using the Gamma function, we can express the product in the numerator and denominator:
   \[
   1 \cdot 4 \cdot 7 \cdot \ldots \cdot (3n-2) = 3^n \frac{\Gamma\left(n + \frac{1}{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)}
   \]
   \[
   2 \cdot 5 \cdot 8 \cdot \ldots \cdot (3n-1) = 3^n \frac{\Gamma\left(n + \frac{2}{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)}
   \]
   Therefore,
   \[
   a_n = \frac{\Gamma\left(n + \frac{1}{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(n + \frac{2}{3}\right)} \cdot \frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)}
   \]
   Using the asymptotic behavior of the Gamma function, \( \frac{\Gamma(n + a)}{\Gamma(n + b)} \sim n^{a-b} \) as \( n \to \infty \), we get:
   \[
   a_n \sim n^{\frac{1}{3} - \frac{2}{3}} = n^{-\frac{1}{3}}
   \]
   Since \( n^{-\frac{1}{3}} \to 0 \) as \( n \to \infty \), it follows that \( a_n \to 0 \).

4. **Raabe's Test**:
   For the product terms \( \left(1 - \frac{1}{3k-1}\right) \), Raabe's test gives:
   \[
   \lim_{n \to \infty} n \left(\frac{1}{1 - \frac{1}{3n-1}} - 1\right) = \lim_{n \to \infty} n \left(\frac{3n-1}{3n-2} - 1\right) = \lim_{n \to \infty} n \left(\frac{1}{3n-2}\right) = \frac{1}{3}
   \]
   Since \( \frac{1}{3} < 1 \), the product converges to 0.

Thus, the sequence \( a_n \) converges to 0.

\[
\boxed{0}

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple banking system in Python. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Create Account**: Create a new bank account with an initial deposit. The account number should be unique and auto-generated.
2. **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to an existing account.
3. **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from an existing account, ensuring that the account has sufficient funds.
4. **Balance Inquiry**: Retrieve the current balance of a specific account.
5. **List Accounts**: Display all accounts and their balances.

The system should handle invalid inputs gracefully and provide meaningful error messages.

**Input Format:**
- A series of commands, each on a new line.
- Commands can be one of the following:
  - `CREATE <initial_deposit>`: Create a new account with the specified initial deposit.
  - `DEPOSIT <account_number> <amount>`: Deposit the specified amount into the account with the given account number.
  - `WITHDRAW <account_number> <amount>`: Withdraw the specified amount from the account with the given account number.
  - `BALANCE <account_number>`: Check the balance of the account with the given account number.
  - `LIST`: List all accounts and their balances.

**Output Format:**
- For each command, output the result of the operation or an appropriate error message.

**Sample Input:**
```
CREATE 100
DEPOSIT 1 250
BALANCE 1
WITHDRAW 1 50
LIST
WITHDRAW 1 300
CREATE -50
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Account created successfully. Account number: 1
Deposited $250.00. New balance is $350.00.
Current balance for account #1 is $350.00.
Withdrew $50.00. New balance is $300.00.
Listing all accounts:
Account #1: Balance = $300.00
Insufficient funds.
Initial deposit cannot be negative. Account not created.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    account_count = 0

    def __init__(self, initial_deposit=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with an initial deposit.
        """
        BankAccount.account_count += 1
        self.account_number = BankAccount.account_count
        self.balance = initial_deposit

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            return f"Deposited ${amount:.2f}. New balance is ${self.balance:.2f}."
        else:
            return "Deposit amount must be positive."

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                return f"Withdrew ${amount:.2f}. New balance is ${self.balance:.2f}."
            else:
                return "Insufficient funds."
        else:
            return "Withdrawal amount must be positive."

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Retrieve the current balance of the account.
        """
        return f"Current balance for account #{self.account_number} is ${self.balance:.2f}."

    def account_info(self):
        """
        Get a string representation of the account information.
        """
        return f"Account #{self.account_number}: Balance = ${self.balance:.2f}"


class BankingSystem:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initialize the banking system with an empty dictionary of accounts.
        """
        self.accounts = {}

    def create_account(self, initial_deposit=0):
        """
        Create a new bank account with an initial deposit.
        """
        if initial_deposit < 0:
            return "Initial deposit cannot be negative. Account not created."
        new_account = BankAccount(initial_deposit)
        self.accounts[new_account.account_number] = new_account
        return f"Account created successfully. Account number: {new_account.account_number}"

    def deposit(self, account_number, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into an existing account.
        """
        if account_number in self.accounts:
            return self.accounts[account_number].deposit(amount)
        else:
            return "Invalid account number."

    def withdraw(self, account_number, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from an existing account.
        """
        if account_number in self.accounts:
            return self.accounts[account_number].withdraw(amount)
        else:
            return "Invalid account number."

    def balance_inquiry(self, account_number):
        """
        Retrieve the balance of an existing account.
        """
        if account_number in self.accounts:
            return self.accounts[account_number].get_balance()
        else:
            return "Invalid account number."

    def list_accounts(self):
        """
        List all accounts and their balances.
        """
        if self.accounts:
            return "\n".join([acc.account_info() for acc in self.accounts.values()])
        else:
            return "No accounts available."


# Example usage of the banking system
bank = BankingSystem()
commands = [
    "CREATE 100",
    "DEPOSIT 1 250",
    "BALANCE 1",
    "WITHDRAW 1 50",
    "LIST",
    "WITHDRAW 1 300",
    "CREATE -50"
]

for command in commands:
    parts = command.split()
    if parts[0] == "CREATE":
        print(bank.create_account(float(parts[1])))
    elif parts[0] == "DEPOSIT":
        print(bank.deposit(int(parts[1]), float(parts[2])))
    elif parts[0] == "WITHDRAW":
        print(bank.withdraw(int(parts[1]), float(parts[2])))
    elif parts[0] == "BALANCE":
        print(bank.balance_inquiry(int(parts[1])))
    elif parts[0] == "LIST":
        print(bank.list_accounts())

+    def tag_resources(self<#sep#>You are given a string that may contain HTML tags, extra whitespace, and mixed case letters. Your task is to write a function `clean_text` that processes the string by removing all HTML tags, converting all characters to lowercase, and eliminating any extra whitespace. The function should utilize regular expressions for the removal of HTML tags.

**Input:**
- A single string `text` (1 ≤ len(text) ≤ 10^4) which may include HTML tags, extra spaces, and mixed case letters.

**Output:**
- A single string with HTML tags removed, all letters in lowercase, and no extra whitespace.

**Sample Input:**
```
"<p>This is a <b>sample</b> paragraph.</p>"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
"this is a sample paragraph."
```

```python
import re

def clean_text(text):
    """
    Removes HTML tags, converts text to lowercase, and removes extra whitespace from the input string.

    :param text: A string that may contain HTML tags, extra whitespace, and mixed case letters.
    :return: A processed string with HTML tags removed, all letters in lowercase, and no extra whitespace.
    """
    # Remove HTML tags using regular expressions
    clean = re.compile('<.*?>')
    text = re.sub(clean, '', text)
    
    # Convert text to lowercase
    text = text.lower()
    
    # Remove extra whitespace
    text = ' '.join(text.split())
    
    return text

# Example usage
input_text = "<p>This is a <b>sample</b> paragraph.</p>"
result = clean_text(input_text)
print(result)  # Output: "this is a sample paragraph."
